A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Hill-Climbing Agent Quality with Agents CLI Evaluation

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: How often do you buy a new car?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We're a two car household and I'm thinking about buying a new car every 3-4 years and keeping it for 6-8 years. The frugal side of me doesn't want to spend around $40k on a new car every 3-4 years but (assuming financial disaster doesn't strike) it won't make any substantial difference in the long run. I'm more concerned about reliability, fewer repairs, newer tech, safety, etc at this point.

Anyway, how do you decide when to buy a new car?
Are you leasing? If so, every 3 years seems like the right cadence. If "it won't make a substantial difference in the long run", why wouldn't you be leasing?

Skooner turns a Kubernetes service-account token into a dashboard session

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Skooner is a Kubernetes dashboard that can read and change cluster resources. Its install guide supports service-account tokens, OIDC, and a NodePort access path. The token that a user enters can inherit substantial cluster authority. A ZoomEye title count is useful for finding possible dashboards, but the token's role bindings and lifetime are the security decision.

On 2026-11-11 at 19:48 UTC, title="Skooner" returned 1,571 ZoomEye matches with sub_type=all, page 1, and page size 1. The application field returned zero, the body field returned 1,574, and the legacy title="k8dash" candidate returned 64. The product's former name is a reminder that inventory queries need a rename check.

The search is available at ZoomEye. A title match does not prove that a cluster is reachable or that the token exposed on the login screen has cluster-admin rights.

The installation instructions describe a service account and a token secret. A user copies that token into the dashboard login. The dashboard server proxies requests to the Kubernetes API. If the service account is bound to a broad role, every session using the token inherits that authority.

Create a dedicated service account for the dashboard and bind only the roles the operators need. Prefer OIDC and short-lived credentials when the cluster supports them. Do not reuse the cluster-admin token from a bootstrap process as the daily dashboard login. Test read, list, create, and delete operations separately for each role.

The install guide offers a NodePort option for quick access. A NodePort is a network exposure decision, not just a service configuration. Restrict it with network policy, a firewall, or an authenticated proxy. If the dashboard is only for a few administrators, a private address or port-forward is safer than publishing the NodePort.

Test from an untrusted network and confirm that the login page and any API routes are not reachable. Then test from the approved administrative network. Record the ingress path, TLS termination, and authentication method so that a later service change does not silently remove them.

The dashboard can update YAML, scale workloads, and manage resources. Enable Kubernetes audit logging and review the service account and user identities that appear in the logs. Alert on token creation, role-binding changes, secret reads, and resource changes made through the dashboard namespace.

The 1,571 title matches are not a count of vulnerable clusters. They are a seed for checking token scope and network placement. Skooner is useful when its login token is a narrow, expiring identity and the dashboard is kept on an administrative path.

Google introduces Gemini Decisions API that scores 1,000 closed-set choices in a single pass

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

📅 November 11, 2026 • Google blog — Google introduced the Gemini Decisions API, a new endpoint for fast closed-set decisions, powered by a new model, Gemini Decision 1 (gemini-decision-1), via the Gemini API in Google AI Studio. Instead of generating answer text token by token, Gemini Decision 1 scores candidate options in a single forward pass and returns probabilities plus confidence scores. One request carries a shared multimodal input across text, images, audio, video and PDFs of up to 256K tokens, with up to 1,000 independent questions evaluated against it.

Three question types, one confidence score Questions come in three types: a yes/no probability called noul, multiple choice with 2 to 255 options returning normalized probabilities for every candidate, and ordered rubric scoring returning a continuous weighted score with per-level probabilities. Every answer includes a confidence score from 0 to 1, so code can route ambiguous cases to a fallback or a human reviewer. Purpose-built use cases named by Google are tool selection, policy checks, model routing and context grading. The confidence score lets code gate on certainty.

Latency and pricing Google reports end-to-end latency of 150–250 ms, median model compute of 50–100 ms contexts up to 16K tokens, with latency nearly flat from 1 to 100 questions per request. Pricing: USD 0.10 per 1M input tokens with zero output tokens billed. Per Google, this makes it nearly 5x cheaper across a shared benchmark suite than Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite with the Interactions API. A no-cost tier exists in Google AI Studio. Per Google, it leads benchmarks in routing, reasoning, ranking and multimodal understanding with the lowest calibration error.

Where Google runs it and what changes Per Google, internal uses include dynamic model routing in Gemini Notebook with up to 40 percent lower latency for daily queries, wireframing in Stitch, emoji selection in CC from more than 200 options, and a safety layer for MCP tools in Google Search. Integrations are rolling out with OpenRouter, LangChain, Vercel AI SDK, LiteLLM, Pydantic AI, Pipecat, LiveKit and OpenCode alongside the google-genai SDKs.

IMPACT — for agent builders: one-pass scoring changes the cost of routing, ranking and policy checks inside agent stacks.

Scores arrive in one response. Sources: blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/developers-tools/gemini-decisions-api x.com/sundarpichai/status/2120581155078729728 mezha.net/eng/news/mezha-c06fca3b9549fb95708df0f2_google_introduces_gemini

Replit Awarded 2026 Microsoft for Startups Partner of the Year

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SAN FRANCISCO, Nov. 11, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Replit, the agentic software creation platform used by builders in 85% of the Fortune 500 companies, today announced it has been named the 2026 Microsoft for Startups Partner of the Year. The company was honored among a global field of top Microsoft partners for demonstrating excellence in innovation and implementation of customer solutions based on Microsoft technology.

"Replit's mission is to give everyone the ability to build software without learning to code, and we're honored to receive this recognition from Microsoft," said Jeff Burke, VP of Partnerships at Replit. "Our work together is making it easier for companies to build and deploy software using the cloud infrastructure and enterprise tools they already rely on. It's an important step toward our vision of the self-driving company where people set direction and AI agents handle more of the execution."

Replit's collaboration with Microsoft combines AI-powered software creation with Microsoft's cloud infrastructure, enterprise data and tools, enabling businesses to build applications while maintaining security, governance and control.

The Microsoft Partner of the Year Awards celebrate partners delivering exceptional customer outcomes through Microsoft Cloud, AI, services, and devices. This year's winners and finalists were selected from more than 4,500 nominations submitted worldwide across a diverse range of categories and geographies. Replit was recognized for providing outstanding solutions and services in Microsoft for Startups.

"Congratulations to the winners and finalists of the 2026 Microsoft Partner of the Year Awards," said Nicole Dezen, Chief Partner Officer and Corporate Vice President at Microsoft. "This year's honorees are harnessing the power of Microsoft Cloud and AI to deliver transformative solutions, accelerate customer success, and expand what's possible. Their ingenuity and impact inspire us, demonstrating what can be achieved when bold vision and technology come together to empower organizations around the world."

The 2026 Microsoft Partner of the Year Awards are announced ahead of Microsoft Ignite, which will be held in San Francisco from November 17-20.

About ReplitReplit is the agentic software creation platform that enables anyone to build applications without learning code. Founded in 2016, the company has more than 60 million users worldwide, including users at 85% of the Fortune 500. Teams at enterprises including Atlassian, Accenture, Adobe, and Databricks use Replit to build apps, and the company has partnerships with Stripe and Slack, among others. Replit is headquartered in San Francisco with offices in London and Tokyo.

Stripe approved the payment. That's not the question a license-key store needs answered.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Here's a fraud scenario physical ecommerce rarely has to think about.

Someone buys a game key with a stolen card. The key is revealed instantly. Within minutes it's redeemed, or resold on a grey-market site.

Weeks later the chargeback arrives. You refund the money. You don't get the key back. No package to intercept, no return to process, no address to flag. The product left the moment the page loaded.

That's daily life for Increddy, a license-key storefront, and it's why "Stripe handles fraud" wasn't enough.

Payment processors rank digital goods among the highest-risk categories, and the reasons stack up:

Every risk decision that matters has to happen before checkout completes. Afterwards, you're just counting losses.

To be clear: Stripe's fraud tooling is good, and it stays in Increddy's stack. It scores the payment (card, device, transaction pattern) against signals from across Stripe's network.

It doesn't answer "is this payment risky **given that the product is an instantly resellable key we can never claw back?"

Those are different questions, and a key store needs both answered.

The design choice that matters isn't any single fraud rule. It's where the risk check sits.

A check that runs after Stripe approves is only reacting to a decision that's already been made. So Increddy's risk scoring is a first-class Medusa module inside the checkout flow with the authority to block checkout completion on its own, whatever Stripe returned.

It gets a vote before the order exists, not a note attached to a transaction that already happened.

You might have noticed there's no list of signals or thresholds in this post. That's deliberate.

It's the same reason banks don't publish their fraud rules: a risk system works partly because the people it targets can't read the rulebook. The architecture is worth sharing. The heuristics aren't, because publishing them would make them worse at their job.

Treat fraud risk as a property of the product category, not only the payment. If what you sell is valuable the instant it's delivered and impossible to take back, give your own risk logic a real seat at checkout, with real veto power.

The fraud layer was one of several custom modules in Increddy's move off Shopify. See the full case study on kryeo.com →

The longer version of this post is on the Kryeo blog.

We're Kryeo. We build checkout and risk logic for headless stores through our Medusa development work.

What 20,858 visitors did on a clothing store in 30 days (and what I'd fix first)

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 11 • Originally published at catalystlabs.co.in

I build websites and web apps at a small studio in Chennai, India. This is a look at the first month of campaign traffic on one store we built, with the real numbers, my reading of them, and the parts I'm unsure about. I'd like your opinions more than your applause.

INEGO is a clothing brand. We built its online store, including a virtual try-on, and we handle the technical support. The ad campaigns were run by ScaleUp Marketing Agency, not by us.

For the last 30 days to early November 2026, Vercel Analytics (production site) shows:

Where visitors came from (rounded, with each platform's mobile and desktop domains combined):

The referrer data can't separate paid from organic visits, so I can't tell you which campaign did what. Traffic came as a sharp spike around the main campaign days, then settled to a steady base.

Evidence: the offers page was the second most visited page, ahead of every category page.

The brand reported more than ₹5 lakh in revenue. That figure is theirs, it covers no stated period, and I haven't audited it. It reflects the brand and its campaigns as much as the store. I'm also working from one client and one month, so please treat all of this as one data point.

I'll reply to every comment, and I'll write up the fixes with before-and-after numbers once they're live.

I Deployed My First Django App on Shared Hosting and Broke It 7 Times. Here's What Fixed It.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

description: The real, unfiltered story of getting Django live on cPanel/DirectAdmin-style hosting, with the exact mistakes and fixes. tags: django, python, webdev, beginners cover_image: The "It Works on My Machine" Lie My Django app ran perfectly on localhost:8000. Beautiful pages. Zero errors. I felt like a genius. Then I uploaded it to a real server. 500 Internal Server Error. No message. No hint. Just a white page staring at me like it knew what I'd done. If you've ever lived this moment, this post is for you. Here are the 7 ways I broke my deployment, in order, so you can skip straight to the fix. Break #1: Debug Mode Was Hiding the Truth On my laptop, DEBUG = True showed me friendly error pages. In production, DEBUG = False showed me nothing. The fix: Check your server's error log first, before touching any code. The answer is almost always sitting there in plain text.

DEBUG = False ALLOWED_HOSTS = ["yourdomain.com", "www.yourdomain.com"] 💡 Forgetting ALLOWED_HOSTS is the #1 reason a fresh deploy fails. Break #2: My Static Files Vanished The site loaded, but with no CSS. It looked like a 1998 homepage. Django doesn't serve static files in production the way runserver does. You have to collect them: STATIC_URL = "/static/" STATIC_ROOT = BASE_DIR / "staticfiles" python manage.py collectstatic Then point your web server at that folder. Suddenly, colors. 🎨 Break #3: Wrong Python Version My laptop ran Python 3.12. The server defaulted to 3.8. Cue a dozen cryptic syntax errors. The fix: Create the app with the right Python version in your host's "Setup Python App" panel, and always pin your versions: pip freeze > requirements.txt Break #4: Secrets in the Code (Please Don't Do This) I almost pushed my SECRET_KEY to GitHub. Almost. import os SECRET_KEY = os.environ.get("DJANGO_SECRET_KEY") Move secrets into environment variables. Your future self will thank you. So will your bank account if you ever add payment keys. Break #5: The Database That Wasn't There SQLite worked locally. On the server, I hit migration errors and file permission problems. Two lessons: Run python manage.py migrate on the server, not just locally. For anything serious, switch to MySQL or PostgreSQL and keep credentials in environment variables. Break #6: The Passenger/WSGI Confusion Shared hosts don't run runserver. They need a WSGI entry point that tells the server where your app lives. If your app won't start, check that your startup file points to the right module: from myproject.wsgi import application One typo in the project name = instant 500. Ask me how I know. Break #7: I Never Restarted the App I fixed the bug. Refreshed. Same error. I fixed it again. Same error. For an hour. The app was still running the old code. Every time you change something on the server, restart the application. It's a button. It's always been a button. My Deployment Checklist (Steal This) [ ] DEBUG = False [ ] ALLOWED_HOSTS set correctly [ ] Secrets in environment variables [ ] collectstatic run [ ] Correct Python version selected [ ] requirements.txt installed on the server [ ] Migrations applied [ ] App restarted [ ] Error logs checked first when something breaks The Real Lesson Deployment isn't a separate skill from coding. It's the moment your code meets reality. Every error I hit taught me something about how the web actually works: what a server does, where files live, why configuration matters. Breaking things 7 times made me a better developer than 7 tutorials did. So if your first deploy is on fire right now, good. You're learning.

Your Turn 👇 What's the worst deployment bug you've ever hit? Drop it in the comments. I'll bet someone has a story that tops "I forgot to restart the app." And if this saved you an hour of panic, a ❤️ helps other beginners find it.

So, what are the best AI video generators, and how do they compare on price?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We look at the hidden costs of the top 5 options.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AI video generation has matured at an astonishing speed. One minute people were mocking attempts to generate footage of Will Smith eating spaghetti, the next we have tools capable of generating footage for everything from photorealistic commercials and animated explainers to social media ads and cinematic short films, all based on simple text prompts.

But choosing the best AI video generator for you isn't only a question of visual quality. The increasing number of models available have varying limitations and strengths and weaknesses in different areas. Pricing structures can also vary wildly, and subscription fees often represent only a fraction of the cost.

Many AI video generators operate on credit systems, charge extra for premium models, or limit video length and resolution in ways that can quickly inflate your spend. With that in mind, here we take a deeper dive into the five best AI video generators available today, which we've ranked according to overall value as well as their flexibility and practical usefulness for professional creatives.

Whichever model or models you choose to work with, you'll probably want one of the best laptops for video editing or the best desktop PCs for video editors.

Adobe is already a powerhouse in filmmaking thanks to Premiere, which we rate as the best video editing software. As a result, Firefly more than just an AI video generator. Unlike many rivals that function as standalone tools, it integrates into Premiere and the wider Creative Cloud environment, making it significantly easier to incorporate AI-generated footage into existing professional workflows.

The most recent video-generation capabilities are particularly impressive. Prompt adherence is strong, footage tends to look polished and cinematic, and Adobe has invested heavily in making outputs commercially viable for professional users.

As announced at Adobe Max 2026, the native Firefly Video editor has been expanded to make it easier to combine clips with other elements such as images and sound effects, and projects created in Firefly Boards, Adobe's collaborative brainstorming space, can be carried straight into the video editor. There's also Adobe's AI agent, which makes using its software more intuitive and conversational.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

The Adobe name also offers reassurance in terms of safety and reliability. The platform's unlikely to disappear or radically change from one day to the next, and output from the native Firefly Video Models is intended to be safer for commercial use since they were trained on licensed material rather than scaped copyrighted video.

On the other hand, if you do want to use other models, they're here in Firefly itself. Google Veo, Kling, Runway and Luma AI's Ray are all integrated and ready to use in Adobe's platform, so you get not only the top AI video generator on our list but access to the second, third and fourth too!

Pricing: Adobe's Firefly Standard plan costs $9.99 per month and includes 2,000 generative credits, enough for around twenty 5-second AI video clips at 1080p quality. Higher-tier plans increase those allowances. Adobe currently prices 1080p video generation at roughly 100 credits per second, meaning a single 5-second clip consumes around 500 credits.

You can also access Firefly video generation through a Creative Cloud Pro plan, which gives you access to Adobe's suite of apps, including Premiere and Photoshop. Just note that you may need to pay for additional generative credits depending on how much footage you're generating, and partner models have different credit costs.

If pure visual quality is your priority, Google Veo has a strong claim to being the most impressive video model currently available. The platform is particularly good at realistic movement, lighting, camera behaviour and environmental detail.

Veo's ability to generate convincing cinematic scenes has made it popular among advertisers, content creators and filmmakers seeking premium-looking footage without a full production budget.

Pricing: Google offers Veo through consumer subscriptions and API access. For developers, Veo 3.1 costs roughly $0.40 per generated second, putting a typical 5-second clip at around $2.00 before retakes. That's easily the most expensive platform in this comparison, but it also delivers some of the highest-quality output available.

You can also access Veo through Google Gemini, Google AI Studio and through Adobe Firefly (see above). Through Gemini, there is a free tier offering just 3 video generations a day, at the time of writing, Google AI Pro costs $19.99 a month for 1,000 Flow credits, which translates to around 100 Lite videos or 10 high-quality Standard videos at 720p with a watermark. Google Ultra AI takes at $99.99 a month and upwards gives you 25,000 credits a month, enough to generate thousands of draft clips at 1080p.

Runway remains one of the most mature AI video platforms on the market. While some competitors may occasionally produce more realistic footage, Runway excels at putting creators in control. The platform includes advanced camera controls, editing features and creative tools that make it particularly appealing to professionals who want more than simple text-to-video generation.

The video editing features enable you to do things such as isolate a subject and remove the background, colour grading, adjusting the depth of field, making a slow motion version of your footage, and 'Inpainting', which means you can easily remove unwanted objects.

Pricing: Runway has several subscription tiers, ranging from entry-level plans through to professional and enterprise options. The credit-based system rewards efficient users but can become costly during experimentation. The Standard plan starts at $15 per month and includes 625 credits. According to Runway's published figures, that's enough for around 52 seconds of Gen-4.5 video generation. A creator producing multiple social videos every week will likely need the Pro plan at $35 per month or higher.

The Chinese platform Kling has become one of the biggest disruptors in AI video generation. It consistently produces impressive results while undercutting many western competitors on price.

Human movement, character animation and stylised content are particular strengths. For YouTubers, social creators and small businesses, Kling often represents the best balance between quality and affordability.

Pricing: Paid plans start at under $10 per month, and a 5-second 1080p clip generally consumes around 40 credits. That works out to roughly $0.40-$0.60 per clip depending on subscription tier, making Kling one of the most cost-effective premium AI video generators currently available and generally cheaper than Veo and Runway. However, as with most platforms, higher-quality generations consume credits faster.

Not every project needs blockbuster-quality visuals. Sometimes speed matters more than perfection, and that's where Hailuo shines. The platform is built around rapid iteration, allowing creators to test ideas quickly and cheaply. Agencies often use it during concept development before moving final production to more advanced systems.

Pricing: Hailuo is typically among the most affordable options available. Standard plans start between $8 and $15 per month depending on promotions and region. A typical 1080p generation consumes about 50 credits, placing the cost per clip around $0.50-$0.75.

The main trade-off is quality, and you might find yourself generating more clips to achieve a satisfactory result, which can offset the initial cost advantage. Additional editing and post-production work may also be required.

Before diving in to AI video generation, it's worth remembering that subscriptions don't always tell the whole story. A $10-per-month plan may sound inexpensive, but the number of usable clips you can generate could be surprisingly limited.

Making direct comparisons between AI video generators is difficult because some services charge per second of output, while others use credit systems that effectively works out as a cost per clip.

Pricing can also vary depending on which plan you choose and how much you use it. Heavy users are likely to have to pay extra for more credits, and plans are continually in flux. Providers have been making regular changes to their pricing as new models roll out and the competitive landscape changes. The prices being quoted today could easily change in a few weeks' time.

The challenge with any AI video generation is that creating a complete project often requires multiple generations. A single eight-second clip may be excellent, but producing several matching shots for a finished sequence can dramatically increase usage costs. You'll often need to generate dozens of variations before selecting a final clip, turning what appeared to be a modest monthly subscription into a much larger operational cost.

Users will also usually need separate editing software to assemble final productions.

For most creative professionals, we think Adobe Firefly currently represents the most balanced and versatile package while also offering reasonable value. While Google Veo may edge ahead on raw visual realism and Runway offers deeper creative controls, Firefly's integration with professional creative software makes it the most practical choice for real-world production environments, and Firefly does also provide access to Veo and Runway too.

That said, the right tool depends heavily on your needs. Consider Veo for cinematic quality, Runway for directorial control, Kling for affordability and Hailuo for rapid experimentation. Just don't judge any platform by its headline subscription price. In the AI video market, credits, render limits, premium models and editing requirements often matter far more than the monthly fee itself.

Joe is a regular freelance journalist and editor at Creative Bloq. He writes news, features and buying guides and keeps track of the best equipment and software for creatives, from video editing programs to monitors and accessories. A veteran news writer and photographer, he now works as a project manager at the London and Buenos Aires-based design, production and branding agency Hermana Creatives. There he manages a team of designers, photographers and video editors who specialise in producing visual content and design assets for the hospitality sector. He also dances Argentine tango.

Cashmere Walls, Baby Goats, and a Bruce Springsteen Sighting at Loro Piana’s Cashmere of the Year Award

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The invitation to Tuesday evening’s Loro Piana dinner arrived via courier. It was printed on heavy card stock, made out in gold ink calligraphy and sealed with red wax. It was no run-of-the-mill invite, which these days mostly arrive via email or DM, nor was it to announce the usual celebration, like a new shoe or bag. Instead, it celebrated farmers and their fibers—in particular, the farmers hailing from Mongolia who act as the conduit behind the brand’s beloved cashmere clothing and the fibers they reap from their flocks of goats in the mountains.

Now in its 11th year, the Loro Piana Cashmere Award spotlights the finest in cashmere fiber production, and that is meant in the most literal sense. At The Brant Foundation in the East Village, the first sight that greeted guests was three bundles of soft white fiber, illuminated with a dramatic spotlight. These were the winning lots selected for their superior quality, and cultivated by Mr. Siqingbilige and Mrs. Hasiqiqige, a husband-and-wife duo in first place, followed by Mr. Jiyatu and Mrs. Gegentuya in second place, and Mr. Lifuqiang and Mrs. Qiqi in third place. “The herdsmen bring us about 10 million kilos of sheared fiber, and this is the best 100 kilos, which we celebrate for their fineness and length of the fiber,” Pier Luigi Loro Piana, the son of the brand’s founder, Franco Loro Piana, told the room. “That denotes the quality.”

In addition to his role as the company’s deputy chairman, he is widely credited with creating the “baby goat cashmere” that thrust Loro Piana into the upper echelons of luxury when it was first revealed in 2008. “It took me many years to convince the herdsmen not to blend the 3% of baby cashmere fiber with the rest of the older goats,” he recalled. “It required extra work, but the difference was so clear, and it is this measure of quality that creates wealth for this population and attracts younger herdsmen to join them.”

In the room were many Loro Piana loyalists, including Huma Abedin, Gucci Westman, Jeff Koons, and Jessica Springsteen, the accomplished show jumper who serves as an ambassador for the brand’s equestrian line. She was joined by her father, the legendary rocker Bruce Springsteen, who was gleefully received by a room largely filled with Italian, French, and American guests. Evidently, the appeal of The Boss is universal.

Also on hand was Jeremy Strong, who has been outfitted by Loro Piana both on and off screen many, many times, though his first memory of the brand predates his career. “I have a memory of being in Italy and seeing the chicest man I’d ever seen in my life with two wolfhounds in a vintage Maserati convertible, and I saw the Loro Piana red mark on the back of his collar, so that image has forever emblazoned itself in the aspirational sky of my mind—just wanting to be that guy in that car,” he told Vogue.

For this occasion, Strong sported a monochromatic suit in a precise shade of Loro Piana brown-beige, and went on to tour the exhibition that had been built to highlight the farmers and their bounty. It included one room with walls of voluminous, fluffy strands of cashmere fiber which ballooned and wafted toward guests walking by. It quickly began clinging to the arms and shoulders and heads of many partygoers before an army of dashing men would offer to remove it with lint brushes with glossy tortoise handles. (In Loro Piana land, even the lint catchers are a considered detail.)

“I think what I was struck by upstairs walking through the exhibition was the way Loro Piana achieves such specificity by hand in this age of mechanical reproduction, this age where everything is computers and AI, and everything is mechanized and done by a machine,” Strong later added. “There is this care, and tactile beauty, and sort of sweat equity that goes into creating these garments. I find something very moving about that.”

The evening’s dinner—beef carpaccio, a plate of saffron risotto, and a fig tart—set the stage for further reminiscing about Loro Piana vintage finds and making plans to visit Mongolia to see the goats and the herdsmen firsthand, followed by an announcement.

After all these ceremonies celebrating the finest 100 kilos of cashmere fibers, something would finally be made using all of these fibers. “We have kept these winning fibers for the last 11 years, and we will be making a collection using them,” Frédéric Arnault, the company’s CEO, told the room. “It will be very small quantities.”

Pier Luigi Loro Piana and Laura Loro Piana

OpenAI’s personal finance features for ChatGPT expands to Mac app

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Earlier this year, OpenAI introduced ChatGPT Finances. The feature started on the mobile app and website, but it’s been missing from ChatGPT’s desktop app. Starting today. ChatGPT Finances is now available on the Mac as well.

ChatGPT Finances originally launched in beta as a preview feature for paid accounts. It originally required a $100/month plan before coming to the $20/month tier over the summer.

Since October, ChatGPT Finances has been available for all plans, including free users. The only requirement is that ChatGPT Finances is limited to users in the United States.

OpenIA says users “can securely connect your financial accounts to find ways to save, understand your spending, and make sense of your investments—with answers grounded in your finances.”

The feature also supports weekly financial report updates, stock monitoring, voice support, and more. You can learn much more about ChatGPT Finances here.

Elemental Royalty (ELE) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Orion deal and divestiture reshape Elemental's cost base and outlook.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Elemental Royalty Corporation (ELE -4.66%) reported record year-to-date performance while announcing a strategic transition toward a pure-play royalty and streaming model. Management confirmed the acquisition of five precious metal streams and royalties from Orion Mine Finance for $290 million and the divestiture of its royalty generation business. This reorganization is intended to reduce general and administrative expenses by more than 25% and simplify the corporate structure. The company reported record revenue and gold equivalent ounces for the first nine months of 2026, supported by organic growth and contributions from the recent merger with EMX. Management stated that the expanded portfolio provides a pathway to exceed 50,000 gold equivalent ounces by 2030 from existing assets.

Frederick Augustus Ronald Peter Bell: Good afternoon, and good morning, everyone, and welcome to Elemental's Q3 2026 Earnings Presentation. And with me today, I have my colleague, Stefan Wenger, our CFO; and you have myself, Frederick Bell, CEO, to talk you through the slides. In terms of an overview of the portfolio, just to give you a summary at the beginning, the company has had a record year-to-date performance in terms of both revenue and in gold equivalent ounces.

We are -- we have the strongest growth profile from this out to 2030 that we've had in the history of the company, getting to a total from existing producing and development assets of over 50,000 gold equivalent ounces from where we are today and that is already within the portfolio [ depth ]. The portfolio remains precious metals weighted. So that is approximately 2/3 precious gold and silver and 1/3 other commodities, primarily copper. It is diversified across operator counterparty and jurisdiction and we have continued to add to that and further diversify with the most recent transaction we have announced. We also just recently closed on an upsized credit facility.

That is the second time we have upsized our credit facility this year, which means that we were both able to use that for the Orion transaction, but also maintaining financial flexibility and drawing on it going forward. In terms of capital allocation, we have a normal course issuer bid put in place during the year that we utilized particularly over the summer where the share price was low and it gives us that flexibility going forward to use it again at the same time that we have had our first dividend in Q1 and now paid on a quarterly basis going forward.

In terms of the management team, we have strengthened that as well over the course of the year, both on the finance team, on the legal team and adding to our technical team as well as we go forward. And lastly, we have -- after the close of -- the final close of the Orion transaction, we have Tether remaining a cornerstone shareholder of about 27% currently and very supportive of the company's strategy going forward.

So a highlight then in Q3 specifically and it was a very active quarter for us, both in terms of transactions that we announced and completed as well as the continuing improvement to liquidity and the capital markets profile of the company that we have done. And one particular, I think, feature was the sale of the generation unit. And what we're doing here is we are both improving that liquidity for shareholders and it came with the GDXJ inclusion for the first time in September.

And then I think the combination of closing the Vizsla transaction previously announced that we finally completed in September, alongside the acquisition of a large portfolio of 5 producing streams and royalties from Orion and the sale of our generation unit that really simplifies, streamlines the company, reduces our G&A going forward and combined with the increase in revenue from the producing assets within the Orion portfolio and that reduction in G&A going into 2027, it will drive improved free cash flow and better margins going forward into 2027.

So we're really in that quarter, continuing to build out the portfolio, both producing and key development assets in Ruby Hill with i-80 at the same time that we are streamlining the business and reducing G&A going forward. In terms of year-to-date then, what we have done, I think here, it's just under $70 million in revenue for the year-to-date. We have approximately $23 million cash on the balance sheet. We still have ability to draw on our credit facility alongside that. And in terms of gold equivalent ounces, that is just under 15,100 gold equivalent ounces year-to-date, which is a record for the company, both in terms of revenue and gold equivalent ounces.

And I think that importantly, we see really strong growth going ahead from our portfolio as it was and with the addition of the Orion portfolio where we will get some partial contribution for this year and a full year's contribution going into next year alongside Endura's asset in New Zealand that will be in production, expect to have first gold in December. So I won't go through all of these milestones here, but I think we wanted just to highlight how active the company has been over the course of this year, both on transactions and on the corporate front. And I think that Q3 has really been a continuation of that.

And we announced in -- post quarter end, we announced the first close on the Orion portfolio and we're expecting to further progress Carlin East to sell the generation business and have an announcement on that in Q4, along with the final close on the last asset in the Orion portfolio in Q1 2027. In terms of our growth outlook moving forward and I think we just highlighted this earlier, but it is really the -- by far and away, the strongest growth profile that we've ever had in the company.

And you can see what it was prior to the Orion acquisition and then in the silver there, the addition of that portfolio, which is a partial year contribution this year in 2026 and then a full year in 2027 onwards and really wraps up with the contribution from i-80's Ruby Hill, where we have a very material silver stream there. And I think what you will see going into 2027 is you will see alongside that increase in gold equivalent ounces and revenue, you will also see a step down in terms of our G&A and costs that Stefan can expand on a bit further in this call. And that is driven by the sale of our generation unit.

And just as a reminder for everyone, in terms of NAV, that business unit probably represented order of magnitude, 1% or less of our NAV, but it was 60% of the personnel in the company. It was a material number of subsidiaries and in excess of 25% of our G&A as well. And so by doing this transaction and did happen simultaneously. But by doing it simultaneously, we're expanding our revenue, reducing our costs and again, should be improving that free cash flow margin going into 2027. And -- so with that, I will hand over to Stefan to run through the financial highlights for the quarter.

And then Stefan will hand back to me a bit later on to talk to a few of the asset-specific pieces and happy to take Q&A at the end of this call.

Stefan Wenger: Thank you, Fred, and good morning, everyone. Thanks for participating in our quarterly conference call. As you can -- as Fred mentioned, it was a really active quarter. The team is really performing well and we are executing on our strategy for the year. A couple of financial highlights. You can see on a revenue basis and an adjusted EBITDA basis, our results, both for Q3 and year-to-date show a substantial increase compared to the prior year. As you know, in the prior year, we were -- we have not yet merged with EMX. So this really shows the benefit of the merger.

In addition to the strong performance of our assets throughout the year, this is driven by GEOs of about 15,000 year-to-date. And you can see in the bottom there that our guidance, we're well progressing towards our full year guidance that we updated in Q3 with the announcement of Orion. So we're operating very well on all fronts on the financial perspective. As I dig into the financials a little bit more, you can see our revenue is up by 153%. Our adjusted EBITDA is up by close to 170% and we have cash of $23 million at the end of the year.

Thinking about the income statement, the question I get most is what do we expect the G&A to look like for the full year and then going forward. And this year has really been a transitional year. We've experienced higher-than-expected G&A because of our integration costs between Elemental and EMX, but also now we're -- we've spent a significant amount of time and effort in the past quarter with the divestiture of the generative business that Fred mentioned. And as we head into 2027, we're going to see the G&A decrease.

This quarter, we had higher G&A costs specifically because of some severance costs related to the divestment of the generative business and also legal costs as we work to complete that in the fourth quarter. I would expect the fourth quarter costs are still going to be in line with the third quarter. But as we head into 2027, we're going to start to really see the benefits. We're going to see the benefits in a couple of ways. Number one, the royalty generation expenses that you see there that are about $5 million a year are going to go away.

And in addition to that, because of the reduction in head count that Fred mentioned and also streamlining activities that we have within the company for simplification of our business on top of the divestiture of the German business, we're going to see significant savings on G&A. We quoted in our release when we announced the Orion acquisition that our G&A reduction -- or our cost reduction will be $6 million a year. And with those additional simplification efforts that we see, we see that we're going to save well beyond $6 million a year and we'll update that as we enter 2027. From a capital structure perspective, it was also a transitional quarter with respect to our capital structure.

We not only completed the acquisition of Vizsla, but we also -- which we drew $30 million on our line of credit. We also positioned ourselves to go ahead and acquire the Orion portfolio. So we've expanded our credit facility. And as of today, we do have $210 million of debt outstanding and $23 million of cash plus equity investments of about $36 million. So on a debt-to-EBITDA basis, we're about 2.5x net debt to EBITDA, but we see that deleveraging rather quickly. And over the next year, you'll see that nearly have as we go forward. So we see that we have still good availability with the credit facility and the accordion feature of about $90 million.

And as was mentioned, with increasing cash flow from the Orion deal, which for next year is about $20 million at current prices plus the reduction of our G&A in the generative business, we're going to be expanding our cash flow significantly that will support that debt reduction. Just a little more detail on the cash flow. For Q3, you can see we started the quarter with $74 million in cash. We generated $13 million of free cash flow just from our royalties and then invested $53.5 million for the Vizsla acquisition, another $25 million for the addition and enhancement of our investment in Quilla and the Chapi project.

And we drew on the line to fund some of those activities, ending the quarter with $23 million in cash. Just for the full year, you can see all of the activities that Fred outlined. And in addition to what I discussed on Vizsla and Chapi, we had other royalty acquisitions during the period and the draw on the RCF. You can see also we continue to fund our dividend. The dividend, we anticipate continuing to fund that and increase that into the future. And we also bought back some shares when we were in the lower share prices back in June and July. We were active on our NCIB as well.

So you can see quite active on the capital front. And with that, I'm going to turn it back to Fred for a portfolio update and then we'll take questions.

Frederick Augustus Ronald Peter Bell: Thank you, Stefan. And looking here at the global map of our portfolio, I think what stands out is we are really cornerstone now by a number of both international miners such as Lundin, Zijin, but also some Nevada Gold Mines, some specific companies in Australia, Capricorn and Genesis, 2 of the preeminent gold miners there as well as a number of producers and developers such as Vizsla and i-80 and Mansa. And that is a deliberate strategy over the course of the last 12 to 18 months of both diversifying our base, but also improving the quality of the underlying assets and the operators and counterparties who are operating those.

In terms of specific updates related to some of the assets in the quarter, the first 2 that I will talk to are Caserones and Karlawinda. Karlawinda, we had the mine expansion that has been going on over the past year. It was successfully commissioned on budget during the quarter. So towards the end of September, Capricorn announced they had completed that commissioning and we expect it now to be running at the higher approximately 150,000 ounce throughput rate. So about a 30% increase in attributable gold equivalent ounces to Elemental going forward. And you can see it's been pretty consistent in terms of the production historically.

They also earlier this year announced an increase in the resource and the reserves alongside that higher production rate that is going through. And so if you look at an asset like Karlawinda since we acquired it, it has, I think, a similar mine life at the time that we acquired it just on the reserves and not including the resources, which would approximately double it. We've benefited now from both the successful mine build, but also now an expansion of the mine that will deliver an increase in production at no cost to the company going forward and we have the optionality to the gold price.

And so it's a very good example of the embedded value that you have in royalties where we no longer have to contribute to the costs, but the operator, in this case, Capricorn are very successfully continuing to explore, add new ounces to the resources, convert existing resources into reserves and simultaneously expand the mine production so that it is now running at approximately 30% higher throughput rate. The second asset, Caserones. Caserones was the primary reason for lower-than-expected revenue in the quarter.

And you can see there the impact from Q2 to Q3, we had about $3.5 million lower revenue in Q3 than we did in the last quarter and that was due to a combination of storms in Chile. And the first one was very well publicized across the state and impacted a number of mines, particularly copper mines in Chile, and it was followed up by another storm that I think compounded the issues and delayed production in Q3. And I think they have announced since that they are updating their guidance down to the lower number for this year, but expecting production to recover both in Q4 and then getting back to normal.

So in terms of our royalty revenue, look, Caserones is a very long-life asset that in our view, we will be producing for many, many years to come. And I think that, that production there is really delayed and not lost to us in terms of revenue, but that was the primary cause of the lower revenue in the quarter of that -- that's all to Caserones. In terms of the next 3 assets, at Timok, we had again a very good quarter there continuing.

As mentioned previously, we did do a site visit to the mine earlier this year and got an update on both current mining, but also their exploration where they've been exceptionally active and the development of the Lower Zone, which is the major value driver at Timok and that is the porphyry that sits beneath the Upper Zone and they are actively developing and expect that we will have some updates from Zijin in the coming months and year on progress at the Lower Zone in addition to ongoing production from the Upper Zone, which is what we received now and then future plans at the MG Zone, which was the major discovery they made approximately 2 years ago within our royalty area as well.

So Timok remains an asset that will be paying us every year and we expect it to continue to appreciate in value as they get closer towards first production in the Lower Zone and then also looking at future plans for the MG Zone. At Bonikro, we have, again, very consistent quarter with Q2 and Q1 and the area where we received royalty from the pushback on Bonikro. That's expected to be a key part of the mine moving forward, and they are benefiting from some higher-grade areas there as well in the coming months and quarters. So we expect that to continue to perform at those levels for us.

And then lastly, in Leeville, this is Nevada Gold Mines in the U.S., and this is part of the Carlin complex. And we did have an update from the mine site team earlier this year as well for getting a better understanding of the mine plan going forward because our royalty covers part of the mine. And so it will sometimes fluctuate quarter-to-quarter. But again, really positive exploration results they have had in our royalty area in recent times and this is a mine that has been producing now for decades and will continue to do so in our opinion. Big Sky Industrial (BSIN) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Carbon hub construction stays on schedule, with helium sales already locked in.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Big Sky Industrial Inc. (BSIN -3.44%) is transitioning from a small-cap exploration and production firm to an integrated industrial gas and carbon management company. Management reported that Phase 1 construction at the Big Sky Carbon Hub remains on schedule for first production in spring 2027. The company stated that the field side of the project is complete, including gathering lines and operational injection wells. Management indicated that its strategy involves leveraging long-term, take-or-pay helium contracts and the monetization of Section 45Q carbon sequestration tax credits. The company stated that its existing infrastructure and acreage already support future expansion beyond the initial processing facility's capacity.

Operator: Greetings, and welcome to the Big Sky Industrial, Inc.'s Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Call. I will now turn the conference over to Mason McGuire, VP of Finance and Strategy.

Mason McGuire: Good morning, and welcome to Big Sky Industrial, Inc. Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Today's call is being recorded, and a replay will be available on the Investor Relations section of the company's website at bigskyindustrialinc.com. Before we begin, I would like to remind everyone that today's discussion will include forward-looking statements within the meaning of the federal securities laws. These statements are based on management's current expectations and are subject to risks and uncertainties that could cause actual results to materially differ. Please refer to the company's most recent SEC filings, including the Form 10-Q filed yesterday and the Form 10-K for a discussion of these risks.

Statements made on this call speak only as of today, and the company undertakes no obligation to update them. Joining us this morning are Ryan Smith, President and Chief Executive Officer; and Mark Zajac, Chief Financial Officer. I'll now turn the call over to Mr. Smith.

Ryan Smith: Thanks, Mason, and good morning, everyone. The third quarter was about execution. When we spoke in August, I said every structural piece of Phase 1 was in place, engineering, permitting, EPC, funding and offtake, and that what was left between here and first revenue was execution. That is where this team has spent the last 90 days in Houston and in Montana, and we are pleased with where the project stands. The field side of Phase 1 is now built. Our infield gathering and injection lines are complete. Our long lead equipment has been procured, including the power generation that will power the plant. Fabrication of the processing facility is underway, and construction remains on schedule.

We are targeting first production in spring 2027, and our helium is sold from day 1. Here's how I'll walk through the call this morning. Construction and operations first, then a direct update on our MRV application, then our commercial position and the market we're selling into. Mark will take you through the quarter and the capital structure, and I'll come back at the end with what's ahead. I'm going to start in the field because that's where the progress this quarter is most visible. Our infield gathering and injection lines are complete. Those are the lines that bring gas from our production wells to the processing facility and carry CO2 from the plant to the Big Rose sequestration asset.

The production wells are drilled, completed, and ready to tie in, and our Class II injection wells are operational. In other words, the field side of Phase 1 -- gas in, helium purified and sold, and CO2 captured -- is in place and ready for the plant. On the plant, our long lead equipment has been procured, including company-owned power generation. In August, we identified the generators as the longest lead time item on the project. They are purchased, on site, and owning our generation puts plant uptime in our own hands from day 1 while significantly improving our operational costs going forward. Fabrication of the modular processing facility is underway with our EPC partner.

The modular design is a big reason we remain comfortable and on schedule. Most of the build happens in the controlled fabrication environment rather than in the field, and far less can go sideways on site. On-site civil work begins in the coming weeks, and equipment installation runs from late in the fourth quarter into the first quarter of next year. We're targeting first production in spring 2027, and I want to be clear why we framed it as a window. It leaves sensible room for commissioning and for Montana winter, and I'd rather give a window than a specific date.

At initial operations, the plant is designed to produce greater than 14 million cubic feet of high-purity helium and to capture approximately 125,000 metric tons of CO2 a year. Now the MRV, as I know it's on the front of the mind of many of you who have dialed in today, our Big Rose MRV is in the final stage of the EPA's approval process. Big Rose is where we will sequester the CO2 captured in Phase 1 and in future phases. We submitted the plan on October 9, 2025. And because of the federal government shutdown, the agency accepted it on November 12, 2025.

We have addressed the EPA's comments and are awaiting final agency action, and nothing in the agency's feedback has changed our expectation of approval. While the process has taken longer than we optimistically hoped, historically, EPA approvals of comparable plans have generally taken 12 months or longer. So our timing is in line with precedent even if we had hoped for a quicker outcome. The review is detailed by design. It covers how CO2 will be monitored, measured and accounted for, including leak detection and verification of long-term sequestration, and the timing of the final decision rest with the agency.

Our responsibility to this point has been to respond promptly and keep the project moving forward, and that's exactly what we have done. The MRV approval is not a condition to starting Phase 1 plant or to selling helium. Our injection wells are permitted and operational, so we can sequester CO2 from the first day the plant runs. The approval timing does not change our resource base, our plant sequestration capacity or our helium offtake terms. Its impact is when we start generating and monetizing 45Q credits, not on the underlying economics of the project. We are advancing our credit monetization work in parallel with the EPA review so that we're positioned to move forward once approval is received.

Our second plan for Cut Bank was submitted on December 17, 2025. It supports CO2-enhanced oil recovery at our Cut Bank oil field over the longer term as we move through future phases, and it continues to move positively through the EPA process. Now to the commercial side of the platform. Our helium is sold from day 1. The offtake we signed in April is a 5-year, 100% take-or-pay contract with an investment-grade global industrial gas company, 1.2 million cubic feet a month at a fixed plant gate price of $285 per Mcf with CPI escalation beginning March 1, 2028, and the pricing redetermination in year 3.

The buyer handles all transportation, logistics and delivery, so we realize the full $285 with no deductions. Volume risk and demand risk sit with an investment-grade counterparty, not with us. The market we're selling into continues to make our case for us. On October 23, the Department of Energy designated helium a critical material under the Energy Act of 2020, citing its essential role in semiconductor manufacturing and growing supply chain risk. We have said for some time that an American producer of a strategically important industrial gas matters a great deal right now, and that designation puts it in writing. There is no substitute for helium in semiconductors, medical imaging, fiber optics or aerospace.

Domestic supply remains thin, and our redetermination gives us a way to participate in a stronger market. Cut Bank Oil Field keeps doing its job by providing low-decline oil production that supports the platform while we build and the captive CO2 outlet for enhanced oil recovery in future phases. With that, let me hand it to Mark to walk through the third quarter results and the capital structure.

Mark Zajac: Thanks, Ryan, and good morning, everyone. I'll cover 3 things this morning: the quarter's results, including the remediation item and lease operating expense, our liquidity and the financing work Ryan referenced. The full detail is in yesterday's press release and in our 10-Q. Starting with the quarter, revenue was $2 million, up 12% from $1.7 million a year ago as a 30% increase in our average realized price to $63.83 per BOE more than offset lower volumes following our 2025 divestiture. Production was 30,628 BOE, 76% oil. Lease operating expense was $2.1 million compared to $1 million a year ago.

The increase reflects approximately $1.2 million of remediation and related costs from the previously disclosed release of produced fluids from a gathering line in our legacy oil operations, which was identified and remediated during the quarter. We submitted a claim under our general liability policy, which provides $1 million of coverage for pollution cleanup costs after a $100,000 deductible, and we expect to recover up to $1 million of these costs. Because the claim is pending approval, no receivable has been recorded at the quarter end. Excluding these costs, lease operating expense was $0.9 million or $29.71 per BOE, essentially flat with the $29.58 a year ago.

Cash general and administrative expense was $1.6 million, down from $1.7 million a year ago and $1.8 million in the second quarter, primarily on lower accounting and tax professional fees. Adjusted EBITDA, which excludes the $1.2 million of remediation costs, was negative $0.8 million compared to a negative $1.3 million a year ago. Net loss was $3.5 million or $0.07 per share. On liquidity, we ended the quarter with $2.9 million of cash and $14.4 million of total liquidity, including $11.5 million of undrawn capacity under our senior secured credit facility. That facility carries a $20 million borrowing base, no financial covenant testing through the quarter ending March 31, 2027, and a May 2029 maturity.

On financing, we are running 2 complementary processes. The first is a new larger senior secured credit facility. Our existing facility was sized for the start of construction. As we approach first production with contracted helium revenue, we believe the asset supports more borrowing capacity, and a larger facility would expand the capacity and our financial flexibility. Those discussions are underway. The second is monetization of the Phase 1 Section 45Q credits. Under tax law, 45Q credits are transferable, which makes the Phase 1 credit stream a financeable asset in its own right and a potential source of nondilutive capital. Those discussions are progressing, and we will update the market as each process advances. With that, back to you, Ryan.

Ryan Smith: Thanks, Mark. Let me close with what's ahead because the next several months are full. Over the coming months, we have a sequence of milestones, each of which further derisks the project. On-site civil work starts in the coming weeks. Equipment installation runs from late in the fourth quarter into the first quarter, followed by commissioning. Our Big Rose MRV plan is in the final stage of the EPA's process, and our credit facility and 45Q monetization processes are advancing in parallel. First production comes in spring 2027 with our helium sold from day 1. Behind Phase 1 is Phase 2.

Engineering and planning are underway for a second larger plant on the same footprint using the same infrastructure, same permits and same field operations we are putting in place for Phase 1. Our acreage, our permitted wells and our geology already support 2 to 3x Phase 1 capacity with no new land required. We expect to make a final investment decision in the coming months following completion of financing and final plant sizing. Once Phase 1 is operating, Big Sky Industrial is no longer a small-cap E&P. We are an industrial gas producer with a contracted offtake, a carbon management business with policy-backed revenue and a low-decline oil business integrated as a captive CO2 outlet.

Small-cap E&P companies trade at roughly 3x EBITDA, midstream and gas processing at roughly 8x and blue-chip industrial gas companies at roughly 17x or higher. Those aren't our forecasts. They are public market multiples that anyone can verify. Our job is to keep executing the milestones and let the market award it. To put a fine point on the quarter, the field side of Phase 1 is complete, the long lead equipment is in hand, plant fabrication is underway and construction remains on schedule. Our helium is contracted from day 1, and the federal government has now formally recognized it as a critical material.

In August, I said the countdown to commercial operation was measured in months, not years, and there are fewer of them now. I want to thank our team in both Houston and Montana for their execution this quarter, and thank you to our shareholders for their continued support as we move from the build phase into the cash flow phase. The path to first production is clear, and we're executing against our plan. Operator, with that, please open the line for questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our first question comes from Charles Meade with Johnson Rice.

Charles Meade: Good morning, Ryan and Mark, and to the rest of the Big Sky team there. Ryan, thank you for the detail really in the context of that 12-month context for the EPA approval. I think I was aware of that, but I hadn't really focused on it. But I wondered if you could just add a little bit more color and tell us how many rounds of comments you've had from the EPA that you've responded to? And what was the last -- what was -- when did your last round of comments come? How long ago?

Ryan Smith: Yes. Good question. Some of this I probably can't get too granular on. But from a high level, I think the expectation going into this was very similar. I'll appeal to the finance crowd a little bit, but very similar to an S-1 with the SEC, where you submit your project, your proposal on the front end, you get reviewed, the review starts. And then over the allotted expected time period going forward, you get questions, comments that you respond to. And in an ideal scenario, those questions and comments get less and less and less as you move forward.

Our process has mimicked that 100% to where the first round we got, I believe it was the very end of last year, maybe early this year, was the most thorough and then a few back and forth turns in the 4 to 5 comment range that got materially less as we move forward is the blueprint that we expected and that occurred with us. So our last round of comments -- again, this is a ballpark timeline -- was around Labor Day, which internally, our opinion was those comments were extremely light, and we instantly responded to those.

And since then, in the company's opinion, our review has been complete, and nothing in the feedback that we've gotten has reflected otherwise. So it's been -- to answer your question, it's been, call it, 6-plus weeks since that last turn occurred.

Charles Meade: Got it. That's good detail, Ryan. And then on the construction of your plant, I wondered if you could share a few of the items on the critical path to startup that you and the Board are the most focused on.

Ryan Smith: Yes. I mean, I think a high level, right, I'll give you a layup answer. It's really everything, right, just because everything ties into it coming online when we want it to come online. A lot of the long lead time items, and I would say, like, the riskiest items were us getting in the queue for generators, membranes, et cetera, pumps, pipe materials that we've gotten ahead of. So right now, fabrication of the plant is beginning off-site. Civil work is beginning on site. And that's complicated, but on the risk spectrum, this is a very, like, old proven type of processing technology, and clearing a site, et cetera, is pretty simple.

So a lot of the riskiest items are behind us. Just going forward, making sure that all of these fabrication assembling the pieces off-site, moving it on. Unfortunately, weather does play a part in moving it on leads to this spring date. The gathering system was a very important thing for us, obviously, so we can move this gas. You want to have that gathering system done by end of November just because you don't want to be operating up there in the winter for installing a gathering system. That's been completed. So again, every single part, we're extremely focused on.

But right now, it's really just the execution of putting the materials together and making sure that's being done on a timely basis going forward.

Operator: Our next question is from Tom Kerr with Zacks SCR.

Thomas Kerr: Any update on the CapEx numbers for the industrial gas build-out? We're at $15 million in 2026. I guess we have about 3 quarters left of build-out cost. What do those numbers look like? Have those changed? And then related to that, is there a maintenance CapEx number once everything gets built out for the industrial gas properties?

Ryan Smith: So I think we have probably, I'd say, 2 quarters left of spend. I would stick to the number we gave on our Q just because intra-quarter numbers are always weird numbers to give. We have spent a little bit this quarter. So I would add a couple of million dollars to the 3Q 9/30 number. But that's all moving along.

I think the only increase that we've had materially that wasn't originally in the plan is what we announced earlier on us acquiring power generation, which net-net is an upfront capital spend, but over the life cycle of operational costs, you more than make your money back and then some and also aren't exposed to increasing power prices in perpetuity, in my opinion, going forward. And on the maintenance costs, the maintenance costs on the plant are not very significant. Of course, there will be some. It's built into the numbers that we've put out in our investor presentation. So you -- of course, like with any machine, you'll have some maintenance costs.

But that's built into our forecast, our operating expenses and outside of something that's major and unforeseen, it's an extremely low maintenance cost on the plant going forward once it's up and running.

Ryan Smith: Yes. I mean, again, I'll reiterate what you said. There's only so much you can say. And I would say all of our conversations reflect that group.

Every group has a different structure and a different need and a different desire on what they transact on going from annual all the way to the entire pool.
