I Saw the Future of Robotics in a Virtual Duck That Chases My Cat

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Allow me to introduce you to the robot cat butler I’m developing with help from AI.

Using OpenAI’s GPT-6 Sol, I taught a virtual version of Microduck—a cute robot from Pollen Robotics and Hugging Face—to walk around a room and identify any bengal cats in its vicinity. (The clip above shows the robot waddling toward a target feline; its eye view is shown top right.)

I’m still waiting patiently for a physical Microduck—please hurry up and ship it, guys—but simulated behavior is relatively simple, so it should transfer over to the real thing. I plan to have the robot automatically dispense a treat for my pet bengal, Leona, perhaps by bowing its head to release one from a 3D-printed, head-mounted storage chamber.

I was able to code and train the virtual robot in just a few hours, which is, quite frankly, incredible. Previously, this kind of thing might have taken me weeks to figure out. It was possible because OpenAI’s latest models are remarkably good at understanding and manipulating 3D representations of the physical world and then translating that into useful code, agentic actions, and robot training.

Real roboticists are using the latest models to do some amazing stuff. When OpenAI released Astra this past September, University of California, Berkeley professor Ken Goldberg and his students saw demos that showcased its ability to render 3D images. They wondered if it could help with the simulations required to train different kinds of robots. These simulations need to be incredibly detailed and extremely accurate to ensure that sophisticated behavior—a humanoid that can dance or a robot hand that can grasp—will transfer faithfully to the real world.

One member of Goldberg’s lab gave Astra access to an Nvidia tool called Newton, showed it a video clip of a real robot in action, and asked if it could create a simulation of the scene.

“What blew us all away was that within an hour it had produced a full 3D simulation with all the parameters set—the position of the camera, the lighting, the background, all the materials,” Goldberg tells me.

Astra is also able to automate much of the drudgery involved with training a robot in simulation, Goldberg says, by running experiments and examining the results.

This is precisely what I found while training the virtual Microduck. Along with GPT-6 Sol, I used an agentic harness from Nous Research called Hermes and a Nvidia DGX Spark, a personal computer that contains a powerful AI chip.

I’ve previously written about using agents and coding models to configure, program, and debug physical robots. Normally, this kind of work requires significant expertise; modern coding models make it a cinch. But OpenAI’s newest model went far beyond just writing code.

It used a piece of Nvidia software called Isaac Sim to feed instructions to a model that controlled the virtual robot, watch videos of it in action to assess how well things were working, and suggest tweaks to its training regime.

This process allowed me to train the Microduck to walk around without toppling over. I initially tried getting GPT-6 Sol to teach the robot to moonwalk—I fed it a GIF of Michael Jackson sliding across the stage, which it tried to copy—but after hours of effort, it only resulted in a very jaunty backward jog. (The robots don’t have us totally beat quite yet.)

Jim Fan, a researcher at Nvidia who has collaborated with Goldberg, says the way AI can now control robots mirrors how software agents function. LLMS can use models trained to control physical actions—visual language action (VLA) models—just as they access different software on a computer.

This week, Fan, Goldberg, and others open sourced an agentic framework for robots called Aspire, which allows a robot to discover and use new skills. One example: GPT-6 Astra figured out how to use a pair of robot arms to solve a Rubik’s cube.

“It picks it up, tries to twist it, but has the wrong angle,” Fan says. “Then it reasons for a very, very long time, but it eventually is able to solve it because it learns the skills over time.”

At the Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), held in Austin, Texas, this week, Fan gave a keynote speech in which he talked about the incredible realization that AI models can automate a lot of the work needed to have a robot do sophisticated things. Fan showed off a demo in which Astra was put in control of a robot arm and told to paint a picture of the Golden Gate Bridge. Using some paint and brushes nearby, it duly completed the task.

Of course, getting robots to work reliably and efficiently is still extremely difficult, requiring significant engineering expertise. The vision system on my robot duck does not work well enough to be deployed in a real product.

Still, the ease and speed of training robots with AI could soon have an enormous impact on manufacturing and e-commerce, and Goldberg says a number of industrial robotics and automation firms have shown an interest in the approach.

Much like vibe coding a few years ago, we may well see breakthroughs in “vibe robotics” as hobbyists and engineers realize how easy it is to program and train robots. And, assuming I can perfect my robot helper, Leona may never have to go without treats again.

This is an edition of Will Knight’s AI Lab newsletter. Read previous newsletters here.

Google brings the Gemini app to Apple CarPlay

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The chatbot was already available through the Google Maps app.

Continuing on its apparent quest to ensure the chatbot is inescapable on as many screens, speakers and experiences as possible, Google has brought the Gemini app to Apple CarPlay. It's available starting today for all supported Gemini Live languages.

Google previously made the AI assistant available on CarPlay through the Google Maps app. Now, you'll be able to access it just by tapping the Gemini icon on your display. Google said you can pick up where you left off with Gemini in your car or start a new conversation with the chatbot.

The company suggested drivers could use Gemini to prep for a meeting during their commute, catch up on emails, hear their Daily Brief or get recommendations for local places worth checking out in an area. Your conversations will sync with the Gemini iPhone app and remain in your chat history.

The Gemini app is now available directly on Apple CarPlay. iOS users can have free-flowing conversations with Gemini Live right from their car's display, hands-free.

Use Gemini in CarPlay to catch-up on what you have coming up in your schedule, talk through your inbox, or... pic.twitter.com/1s4luZ6q0v

To use the assistant on CarPlay, you'll need to make sure you have the latest version of the Gemini app on your iPhone and the microphone permissions are on. You'll need to have used Gemini Live at least once on your iPhone as well.

Google brought the Gemini app to CarPlay not long after it enabled Personal Assistance (which can access details from other Google apps for more personalized responses) in the chatbot on Android Auto. The company is bringing Personal Intelligence to Gemini on cars with Google built-in as well.

CoreWeave turns to customer prepayments, shorter contracts to fund AI growth

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CoreWeave is rewriting how it pays for the AI boom, according to the company’s third-quarter earnings, reported on Tuesday. CoreWeave, a neocloud that rents out the computing power behind AI, said it’s increasingly asking customers to pay up front, borrowing against specific contracts, and even pursuing short-term deals that could lead to higher prices.

The new strategies could help CoreWeave finance its growth more efficiently while squeezing more cash from existing infrastructure. But these bets only hold if today’s demand and pricing power continue.

CoreWeave reported $3.66 billion in revenue, up 168% from the year prior, but it also posted a $709 million net loss and $846 million in quarterly interest expense.

“We will not use the unsecured debt market to fund our business at current yields, nor do we need to,” CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator said during Tuesday’s earnings call.

Instead, CoreWeave’s plan is to rely on loans backed by specific customer contracts, drawing on funds as needed to finance most of infrastructure it still has to build to deliver on its significant backlog of $132.5 billion.

CoreWeave also said it expects about $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits over the next three months, including more than $3 billion tied to signing new customer contracts for capacity it has already built, which requires no additional spending on hardware.

Rivals Nebius and IREN have also relied on hefty Microsoft prepayments to pay for computing capacity, signaling that the industry is moving toward asking Big Tech to pay up for their buildouts.

Upfront payments could alleviate CoreWeave’s immediate borrowing needs, but they also commit the company to providing services at a price that might be less attractive down the road if compute demand continues to surge.

CoreWeave is also looking to capitalize on that growing demand by signing more shorter-term contracts that can command much higher prices.

The company raised prices across its offerings by about 25% in July and by another 10% later in the quarter. Prices are still climbing this quarter, in a way that “more than offset[s] cost increases,” said Intrator.

“We are also allocating more capacity to short-term opportunities, where the pricing justifies it, selling capacity at more than $40 million per megawatt annually, while pricing for new infrastructure on four and five-year contracts is moving to $20 million to $25 million per megawatt,” the chief executive said.

CoreWeave’s financing strategy now revolves around those opportunities. In August, the company closed a $2.6 billion loan, its first designed specifically for shorter-term customer contracts. Known as DDTL 5.5, it lets CoreWeave borrow in installments as it needs the money.

While short-term deals let CoreWeave charge higher prices and change them more frequently, it’s a tradeoff. Lenders give more weight to longer-term contracts, even if they’re priced lower, because they value predictable cash flow and consistency to repay the loan above all.

Even though Wall Street reacted positively to CoreWeave’s earnings, bond investors are much less sanguine. Last month, yields on some of the company’s bonds hit above 15%, highlighting that investors are concerned about the debt needed to fund data center buildouts before those investments see a return.

In short, CoreWeave is finding new ways to expand without relying on unsecured debt. The strategy it has chosen could boost profits, but it also carries greater risk around contract renewals and pricing — and it just goes to show how dependent the AI infrastructure boom is on sustained, and as yet unproven, demand.

TechCrunch has reached out to CoreWeave for more information about its loan facility.

Argá Medtech Completes Enrollment of Paroxysmal AF Cohort in the COHERENT-AF Pivotal IDE Study

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Prospective, multicenter study evaluates the Coherent Sine Burst Electroporation Ablation Platform, a sine wave-based pulsed field ablation technology, in drug-refractory atrial fibrillation; persistent AF cohort enrollment continues

SAN DIEGO, Nov. 11, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- ArgÃ¡ Medtech, a medical technology company developing sine wave-based pulsed field ablation (PFA) for cardiac arrhythmias, today announced the completion of enrollment in the paroxysmal atrial fibrillation (PAF) cohort of COHERENT-AF, its pivotal U.S. Investigational Device Exemption (IDE) study of the Coherent Sine Burst Electroporation (CSE) Platform. The PAF cohort enrolled 150 intention-to-treat subjects across 27 sites in Europe and the United States. Enrollment in the persistent AF (PersAF) cohort is ongoing and expected to complete in the next two months.

"Completing enrollment in the paroxysmal AF cohort is an important milestone for COHERENT-AF," said Emile Daoud, MD, Director of Electrophysiology Research; Hatton Research Institute at Bethesda North Hospital, Cincinnati, Ohio and the leading enroller in COHERENT-AF. "In our procedures, the CSE Platform has consistently delivered pulmonary vein isolation with a streamlined, single-catheter workflow, and the ability to address the pulmonary veins, posterior wall and cavo-tricuspid isthmus without exchanging catheters has been a meaningful advantage in our lab. We look forward to the completion of the persistent AF cohort and the 12-month effectiveness results."

COHERENT-AF (ClinicalTrials.gov: NCT06784466) is a prospective, multicenter, non-randomized pre-market study evaluating the safety and effectiveness of the CSE Platform in adults with symptomatic, drug-refractory PAF or PersAF. The study plan includes enrollment of up to 410 subjects in the United States and Europe, including 150 intention-to-treat subjects in each of the PAF and PersAF cohorts. Additional subjects will be enrolled in a second phase of the IDE investigation.

All subjects undergo pulmonary vein isolation (PVI) with the CSE system. Posterior wall and other left atrial substrate and cavo-tricuspid isthmus ablation may be performed with the CSE system at the operator's discretion. Each cohort is evaluated separately against prespecified performance goals for safety and effectiveness, with subjects followed through 12 months.

"Reaching full enrollment so quickly in the paroxysmal AF cohort reflects the commitment of our investigators and study teams," said David Neale CEO, ArgÃ¡ Medtech. "We are now focused on completing the persistent AF cohort and generating the evidence needed to support FDA and CE-Mark regulatory submissions of the CSE Platform."

About the Coherent Sine Burst Electroporation Platform

The ArgÃ¡ Medtech CSE Platform features a single ablation catheter that can be configured by a simple stylet exchange to deliver circular, linear and focal ablation lesions. The CSE generator delivers controlled bursts of microsecond sine-waved based pulsed field ablation (PFA) across two, four or eight electrodes and can be titrated to treat the full spectrum of atrial tissue from thin-walled RV to thick septal or intramural circuits.

CAUTION: Investigational device. Limited by Federal (or United States) law to investigational use.

ArgÃ¡ Medtech SA is a venture capital-backed medical technology company dedicated to developing and delivering innovative technology to help patients affected by heart rhythm disorders. Its PFA platform uses sine waves to deliver energy and includes the SineBurstâ¢ Ablation Catheter and the SineWaveâ¢ Ablation Generator. The company is based at BiopÃ´le Lausanne in Epalinges, Switzerland, with U.S. operations based in San Diego, California. Nike’s Goal With the Structure Plus? Make Stability Less Boring

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The brand’s classic stability shoe gets the super trainer treatment with a tall stack height, lighter weight, and PEBA added to the midsole.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

How do you change runners’ conception about stability? Last December at The Running Event in San Antonio, Texas, Nike Expert PLM Ashley Campbell held up the answer: the Structure Plus.

“Stability can be boring,” she admitted. The Structure Plus makes it exciting.

The shoe follows the Vomero Plus, a super trainer version of the Vomero 18. The Structure Plus is the super-ified version of the Structure 26. Where the latter is more accessible for $140, the “plus” iteration is the original amplified in every aspect.

The Structure Plus became my chosen shoe for long runs during my 2026 Boston Marathon training cycle. Here’s how the shoe won me over on a 12-miler, and why you should consider trying a stability super trainer.

While developing the Structure Plus, Campbell said the team set out to make the super trainer “more supportive, smoother, softer, and more palatable.”

Tech-wise, you can see how Nike landed on the first three. The shoe’s platform is wider to provide support. A convex flat shape of sidewall provides stability, the density underneath the arch acting as a stabilizer to help with alignment. A dual layer of foams, like in the Vomero Plus, provides a soft, energizing ride.

In terms of making the shoe more palatable, Campbell wanted the Structure Plus to be all-inclusive. “You don’t have to be an overpronator [to run in the shoe.]”

At 42 mm in the heel and 32 mm in the forefoot, it’d make sense if you expected the shoe to be heavy and unstable. But the Structure Plus challenges these notions.

At 10.9 ounces, men’s 10, the shoe is lighter than the Structure 26, which weighs 11.3 ounces. The maximal cushioning is a layer of PEBA-based ZoomX, a soft foam that delivers high rebound (the foam is also in Nike’s premium marathon shoe, the Alphafly), and TPE-based ReactX, a firmer foam that helps stabilize your ride (the same foam found in the Vomero 18).

Despite the high stack height and max cushioning and stability components, the shoe didn’t weigh me down on my runs, nor did it cause my feet to roll. I didn’t feel like I was forced into alignment. The shoes guide you instead of directing your foot.

It was such a contrast after running in the Vomero Plus the weekend before. During those miles, it got to the point where my friends were getting concerned after I tripped over myself for the umpteenth time. My next long run was smooth, all thanks to the Structure Plus for keeping me stable.

I’m a neutral runner, but as the miles pile on, like they do on long runs, my form gets erratic due to fatigue. It’s those later miles where the Structure Plus saves me from hitting the pavement, or worse, benching myself with injury.

Concerning actual overpronators, our testers were satisfied with the support the shoe provided.

“What I liked most about the Nike Structure Plus was the strong sense of support it delivered on every run,” said tester and overpronator Julian Caiazzo. “The shoe felt stable and dependable without ever coming across as stiff or restrictive. It gave me the confidence to settle into my stride, and even with all that structure, the ride still felt smooth and enjoyable.”

Another overpronator, Katherine Thomas, admitted she usually avoids Nike shoes because of their lack of stability. She was pleasantly surprised with the support of the Structure Plus.

If there’s any reason why you should try the Structure Plus, it isn’t just that it’ll change your preconceived notion of a stability shoe; it’ll also change what you think of Nike trainers.

Like Thomas, I also avoided Nike shoes (unless I had to test them) because of their lack of stability and narrow fit. The Structure Plus doesn’t feel like a Nike shoe; there’s no squeezing on the sides of my midfoot, no pinching by my toes. And yet, I reap that quintessential Nike ride, that layer of ZoomX adding more pop to my strides. Those long runs are anything but boring slogs. I can emphatically say that Nike achieved its goal of making stability—and my runs—more exciting.

Shop Men’s at nike.com | Shop Women’s at nike.com

Amanda Furrer, Editor, Running Reviews, studied journalism at NYU and writing at Emerson College. She has reviewed gear and covered other topics in the running space for almost 10 years. Since 2013, she has consecutively run the Boston Marathon. She also has a master’s degree in gastronomy from Boston University and was formerly a professional baker for two years before hanging up her apron.

"It's just wild the talent that they let go"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nothing zaps the joy right out of launch day celebrations like getting laid off alongside half of your team, but that's exactly what happened to dozens of Id Software developers during Microsoft's July 2026 bloodbath (not to be confused with its September 2026 bloodbath) alongside the release of the Revelations DLC for Doom: The Dark Ages. Amid reports that the studio was gutted down to support studio size, the total confirmed number of laid off employees, per Game Developer, was 136 as of this past July, which current studio leadership has confirmed was about half of the team. That's an absolutely devastating loss of institutional talent, but a group of former Id Software devs has risen from the ashes as new worker-owned studio Hidden Horses. In the studio's announcement documentary, they don't hold back.

"It's just wild the talent that they let go. It's just wild that they would attack Id like that the day before we release our DLC. It really felt like an attack," says Hidden Horses co-founder and former Id Software tools programmer John-Michael Quinlan.

"It was probably emotionally the worst day of my life," says former Id Software senior technical designer Jordan Turner, who's now co-founder and, fittingly, technical designer at Hidden Horses. "I don't think I've cried that much in my entire life."

"I did not see it coming at the time that I was told," says former Id Software programmer, now Hidden Horses rendering programmer, Regan Carver. "I just told my wife, 'well that was not a fun conversation.' She thought I was joking at first, and then she realized I wasn't. I was in shock, but she was in tears.

"I usually spend the release days scouring social media looking for all the good news. It drives up my morale ... I'm always excited to see people receiving our stuff well and knowing I was a small part of it, so that was gone."

Amid a period of historic tumult in the gaming industry, it's easy to become desensitized by news of another mass layoff, but hearing from the workers themselves in this way really underscores the immense human toll of corporate downsizing.

Former Id Software AI engineering lead Daniel Young says the hardest part about being laid off was losing friends he'd worked with for years. "It hit me pretty hard. I think that was the biggest thing was the people that I was gonna be leaving behind. I hate to say it's like a death in the family, but it's almost like that's what it felt like," says Young.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

"Personally the biggest thing was that I'm trying to help my kids through college," says Todd Boyce, former Id Software lead VFX artist and Hidden Horses technical artist. "I have two kids in college right now, and that is really expensive. That's probably the thing that hurts me the most is the possibility of them being financially stressed because of something that wasn't even in their control, and for a layoff that honestly didn't need to happen."

Hidden Horses is a small studio of 19 developers according to this announcement video, and its Kickstarter describes its debut title, Danger Pay, as "a single player FPS based around a deadly gameshow with high-octane action and gameplay focused design." Meanwhile, Hidden Horses producer Andrew Willis says players can expect "absolutely stellar out-of-this-world fun gameplay, amazing and super cohesive and stylistic visuals, and really optimized gameplay on the technical side."

Technical designer Tim Locke says one key difference between working at a corporate-owned studio like Id and now Hidden Horses is creative freedom. "Being able to do what we want and talk through, like, what are our ideas, like, which ones resonate with the most people and how do we move forward with that. That's really the difference, and it has been different so far. It's been great."

Echoing that sentiment, Hidden Horses lighting artist Tyler DeLisle says "the people that were let go at Id ... we were all, none of us were slackers. We were all best of the best, top of our game, so now that we're all given this opportunity to come together and keep doing what we love to do, but kind of untethered and more collaborative, I think we can really put out something special."

"If even a small measure of those games that we've worked on in the industry, for me over the past 20 years, is able to be distilled down into a game for our fans, I think they're gonna get something really special," says Quinlan. "And I think you only have to look at the pedigree of the people involved to know that we can do something really special here."

Inside Xbox, "the only concrete thing they have communicated is they want higher profits, and they want it now," says id Software employee

Sen. Warren, Merkley raise ethics concerns over Trump aide’s prediction market bets

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

In August, the CFTC ordered Gabriel Perez to pay more than $172,000 after he allegedly read Trump's prepared remarks shortly before delivery and placed his bets on Kalshi mention markets.

Democratic Sens. Elizabeth Warren and Jeff Merkley are demanding answers from the White House after its teleprompter operator was accused of using advance information to place bets on President Donald Trump's speeches.

The lawmakers say that the White House has failed to take "meaningful action to address ethics concerns," in a letter to White House Counsel Will Scharf.

"We are writing to request information about how the White House is responding to allegations from earlier this year that a key presidential aide enriched himself by abusing access to nonpublic information, and how this alleged incident is shaped by the White House’s lax ethics and anticorruption policies and practices," they said.

In August, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission ordered Gabriel Perez to pay more than $172,000 after he allegedly read Trump's prepared remarks shortly before delivery and placed his bets on Kalshi mention markets, which try to predict specific words that public figures will use. Perez's situation is one of several in which people have used inside information to place bets on prediction markets, including an active-duty U.S. Army soldier who used confidential information to place bets on Polymarket ahead of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro’s capture earlier this year.

Warren is set to take on a larger role after Democrats took control of the Senate and House in last week's midterm elections. She will lead the Senate Banking Committee, where she has said she would seek to "end the current Wild West-style lack of oversight in this area, with corrupt insiders profiting." Another Democrat, Rep. Ro Khanna, who is weighing a 2028 presidential bid, called for a ban on sports prediction markets on Tuesday.

In the letter, Warren and Merkley said that the White House needs to do more than send a warning memo and take heed of what other administrations have done, including mandatory training.

"More action is required—from the President, from you, and from other White House officials—to provide the public with confidence in the integrity of the federal government under President Trump," the senators said.

Specifically, Warren and Merkley said they want information on whether other employees had "misappropriated insider knowledge to place any bets, trades, purchase contracts, or taken any similar action for personal gain," and if training is available for nonpublic information.

The White House did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Watch Out Hollywood: Creators and Sports Are Eating More of the Content Budgets

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A new report from KPMG shines a lot on where the content spend is flowing, and traditional film and TV is losing out.

Where the entertainment business spends its content budget is shifting, with sports and independent creators reaping the rewards.

The consultancy KPMG released its annual media content spend analysis on Thursday, calculating the totals and outlining where the major entertainment platforms (like Disney and Netflix, but also YouTube, Meta and Amazon) are spending their money. Traditional film and TV content still has a role, but the report underscores how the winds are shifting.

The good news? Content spend is continuing to rise. In 2025, the companies KPMG tracks topped $207 billion, up from $156 billion in 2021. KPMG calculates spend based on costs associated with film and TV production, programming and licensing; sports rights; revenue sharing payments to creators; and music costs.

But while most traditional players (Paramount, Comcast, Disney) are seeing their content spend stagnate, it is companies like YouTube, Amazon and Meta where spend is rising.

That is reflected in the data, where YouTube is now king of the spenders, with an estimated $28 billion annually. Creator content now accounts for $46 billion of the annual content pie, up 75 percent from 2021. Sports meanwhile is now 29 percent, up 59 percent from 2021, as places like Netflix, Amazon, Apple and others pour cash into the space.

And of course places like Netflix, Disney+ and others are also cutting creator deals, bringing more types of content into their streaming fold.

“Creator content is taking on a larger role in the content portfolio because it can build frequent engagement, attract highly engaged audiences, and create new opportunities for advertising and monetization,” said Frank Albarella, Jr., U.S. sector leader, media and telecommunications, at KPMG US. “As the lines between creator-led and traditional entertainment blur, the question for media companies is how those formats work together to win consumer engagement and drive value.”

In 2021, film, TV and streaming content accounted for 71 percent of all content spend, but the new KPMG report finds that number sinking to 64 percent, as both sports and creator content surge.

“A successful ‘super app’ starts with a clear view of what your audience values,” said Scott Purdy, media sector leader, strategy, KPMG U.S. “The opportunity is to combine the content, experiences, and benefits that give your customers more reasons to stay, while making that relationship more valuable over time.”

Still, the data shows a shifting landscape for Hollywood, one that newly-enlarged giants like Fox-Roku and Skydance will need to navigate. Premium film and TV remains the heart of Hollywood, but it is careening toward a future where it is a smaller and smaller piece of the puzzle.

Israel Discount Bank Becomes First in Country to Offer Bitcoin and Ethereum Trading

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Discount Bank will launch direct Bitcoin and Ethereum trading through its mobile app.

Customers can trade crypto without opening accounts with external exchanges.

Discount Bank will waive account management fees for the first six months.

Israel Discount Bank, a $150 billion bank, has become the first in the country to offer Bitcoin and Ethereum trading through its mobile app. From November 26, customers can buy, hold, and sell both cryptocurrencies without using a separate exchange or wallet.

This puts the bank ahead of rival Bank Leumi, which plans to launch crypto trading in early 2027.

On November 11, Israel Discount Bank announced that its new crypto trading service will go live on November 26, making it the first Israeli bank to offer direct in-app buying and selling of digital assets.

The service will initially support Bitcoin and Ethereum.

Eligible private customers aged 18 and above can trade the two assets directly from their bank accounts, provided their accounts are enabled for securities activity and they meet the bank’s terms.

Users will not need to open an account with a foreign crypto exchange or manage a separate wallet. Bitcoin and Ethereum will be held through bank wallets using Fireblocks’ digital asset infrastructure, while proceeds from sales can return directly to the customer’s bank account.

Discount Bank CEO Avi Levi called the launch “a first step” toward building the bank’s digital currency infrastructure.

One of the key features is the treatment of crypto taxes. Discount Bank said customers will be able to offset capital gains and losses from digital-asset activity against other investments in their portfolio for withholding-tax purposes.

This keeps the tax calculation within the banking and investment account, rather than leaving customers to handle the process separately.

The bank will also waive account management fees for the first six months. However, trading commissions are reported at 2% per purchase or sale.

The launch could give Discount Bank an early lead as Israeli banks compete to attract customers interested in crypto.

Bank Leumi announced a similar plan in August, allowing customers to trade Bitcoin, Ethereum and Solana through its Leumi Trade app. However, the service is not expected to launch until early 2027, as the bank moves ahead with its partnership with Galaxy Digital.

This is not Leumi’s first attempt to enter crypto trading. In 2022, it planned to launch a similar service with Paxos, but the Bank of Israel blocked the move.

Reed's (REED) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Gross margin expanded and losses narrowed, but management says much work remains.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Reed's, Inc. (REED +15.08%) reported a year-over-year decrease in net sales while expanding gross margins through reduced promotional allowances and logistics optimization. Management prioritized stabilizing the balance sheet by refinancing existing debt and completing a ten million dollar private placement to support working capital and strategic growth initiatives. The company is focusing on a turnaround plan that includes expanding its retail footprint via a national broker network and developing new product lines for 2027. Management stated that operational improvements in delivery and handling costs have partially offset sales volume declines, and they expect sequential financial progress to continue through the end of the fiscal year.

Operator: Good morning, and welcome to Reed's Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call for the 3 months ended September 30, 2026. My name is Annis and I will be your conference call operator for today. Today's call will include prepared remarks from Neal Cohane, Reed's Interim Chief Executive Officer, and Douglas McCurdy, Reed's Chief Financial Officer. Following their remarks, we will open the call for questions. Before we begin, please take note of the company's cautionary statement. Reed's assumes no obligation and does not intend to update these forward-looking statements, except as required by law. For more information, please refer to the Risk Factors section of the company's annual report filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission on March 25, 2026, and in other filings that the company makes from time to time with the SEC. When discussing results, the presenters may refer to non-GAAP measures, which exclude certain items from reported results.

Please refer to Reed's third quarter 2026 earnings release on Reed's Investor website at investor.reedsinc.com and the company's quarterly report on Form 10-Q for the quarter ended September 30, 2026, expected to be available on the website soon, for definitions and reconciliations of non-GAAP measures and additional information regarding results, including discussion of factors that could cause actual results to materially differ from forward-looking statements. While we believe the non-GAAP financial measures provide useful information for investors, the presentation of this information is not intended to be considered in isolation or as a substitute for financial information presented in accordance with GAAP. I will now turn the call over to Mr. Cohane.

Neal Cohane: Thanks, Operator, and good morning, everyone. Third quarter marked another step forward in improving our operating performance with continued progress on gross margins and cost discipline. In short, the turnaround plan is taking hold. The actions we have taken throughout the year are beginning to translate into a more efficient operating structure. Gross margins increased to 25% compared to 24% in the second quarter and 17% a year ago. While operating expenses continue to decline, we recognize that restoring revenue growth remains a priority, and our focus is on building these improvements as we position the business for 2027. As we emphasized last quarter, while we're making progress, there is still considerable work ahead of us.

We've taken steps to address the challenges facing the business, but we are not yet where we wanna be. Let me give you some context on key areas of progress during the third quarter. An important development since our last call is that we have strengthened our balance sheet. At the end of the third quarter, we refinanced our credit facility and extended its maturity to June 2027. After quarter end, we closed a $10 million private placement. Doug will walk you through the details, but the bottom line is that we now have the capital to execute our plan.

With the completion of our $10 million private placement, we have strengthened our financial position and provided the company with additional flexibility to execute our business plan. Our capital allocation priorities are straightforward. First, to support working capital, ensure operational stability; second, to continue improving our cost structure and operating efficiency; and third, we will make targeted investments in our brands and retail partners that will drive incremental revenue and long-term growth. Importantly, we intend to be disciplined and increasingly selective in how we deploy capital. While we will continue to manage expenses carefully, we also recognize that returning Reed's to growth requires investment behind our brands and our retail distribution footprint.

As we move into 2027, we will expect to place greater emphasis on focused marketing, including digital, social media engagement and influencer retailer marketing. This is designed to build awareness, drive consumer demand, which will translate into measurable sales. Our objective is to ensure that every marketing dollar we deploy is backed with a return on investment that will increase velocities while creating long-term shareholder value. On the retail channel side, our investment in a stronger national broker network is beginning to deliver results. Year-over-year, we have secured more than 3,000 new retail doors while also regaining distribution with several important national and regional accounts.

This represents meaningful progress in rebuilding our retail footprint and expanding the availability of our brands. We expect these new placements to begin contributing more meaningfully in the fourth quarter and throughout 2027 as products reach store shelves, gain consumer visibility and build velocity. Our focus is now on supporting these placements with disciplined sales and targeted marketing to convert expanded distribution into sustainable revenue growth. Product innovation will be another important driver of our growth strategy as we move into 2027. We are continuing to build on Reed's strong heritage in ginger with the development of our new mixer line, which we believe creates an exciting opportunity to expand our presence and relevance in the beverage category mixer space.

In addition, we are working towards what we believe could be a game-changing product innovation targeted for launch in early 2027. While we are very excited about the potential of this innovation, we intend to take a measured and disciplined approach to its launch. Our initial strategy will be to introduce the product through a controlled market test with our local customers and with very specific retail partners. This will allow us to evaluate consumer response, product velocity and operational execution in a short period of time before considering a broader rollout. While we're not prepared to share additional details at this time, we are excited about this initiative and what it could mean for the future of Reed's brands.

Our objective is to bring meaningful new innovation to the market that differentiates our portfolio, attracts new consumers and creates incremental growth opportunities with our retail partners. Reed's will return to growth by not only growing our existing portfolio, but by constantly innovating and partnering with key retail partners around the country. Our decades of distribution and retail partnerships are what we do best. We are now leveraging those relationships. It's time to pay attention. On costs, we will continue to balance our selling, general and administrative expenses with the size of the business, and those expenses declined meaningfully both year-over-year and sequentially.

Our operations team has also done a strong job managing the logistics, vendor relationships and challenging -- in a challenging fuel and freight environment with delivery and handling costs down 10% year-over-year. In Asia, we have narrowed our focus and significantly reduced our investment in the second half of the year. We continue to closely monitor our investment there, and we'll update investors as appropriate. Stepping back, the third quarter demonstrated continued sequential progress in gross margin, operating expenses and net loss, but we recognize that substantial work remains, particularly on top line.

As we close out the year and move into 2027, we are focused on disciplined commercial execution, putting our new capital to work, continued margin improvement and positioning Reed's for profitable and sustainable growth. In the fourth quarter, we expect net sales and gross margin to sequentially improve compared to the third quarter and the prior year period. We are not simply focused on stabilizing Reed's. We are focused on rebuilding the future -- rebuilding forward into an exciting growth company. That means we're rebuilding our commercial foundation, investing in meaningful product innovation, reconnecting with consumers and creating new opportunities with our retail partners.

We believe Reed's has tremendous brand heritage and untapped potential, and our focus is on translating those strengths into sustainable growth, improved profitability and long-term shareholder value. I'd like to thank everybody for your time today, and I'll turn the call over to Doug now.

Douglas McCurdy: Thank you, Neal. Turning to our results for the third quarter of 2026. All variance commentary is on a year-over-year basis, unless otherwise noted. Net sales for the third quarter of 2026 were $6.5 million compared to $7.0 million in the prior year period. The decrease was primarily driven by lower volumes with recurring national customers, partially offset by lower promotional and other allowances. Gross profit for the third quarter increased 34% to $1.6 million compared to $1.2 million in the prior year period. Gross margin expanded to 25% compared to 17% in the prior year period.

The increase was primarily driven by lower promotional and other allowances and lower cost of goods per case, which decreased to $13.26 from $13.73 in the prior year period. Delivery and handling expenses decreased by 10% to $1.0 million during the third quarter of 2026 compared to $1.1 million in the third quarter of 2025, primarily driven by the net sales decrease and continuing improvements in logistics efficiency and freight optimization. Delivery and handling costs were 15% of net sales or $2.69 per case compared to 16% of net sales or $2.60 per case during the same period last year. Selling, general and administrative expenses decreased 15% to $3.6 million compared to $4.2 million in the prior year period.

The decrease was primarily driven by continuing efforts to optimize selling, general and administrative expenses, partially offset by investment in personnel and related services to support our Asia growth initiative. Net loss during the third quarter of 2026 improved to $3.2 million or negative $0.27 per share compared to a net loss of $4.0 million or negative $0.48 per share in the prior year period. EBITDA loss improved to $2.9 million in the third quarter of 2026 compared to an EBITDA loss of $3.6 million in the year-ago period. Turning to liquidity. Cash used in operations improved to $0.3 million in the third quarter of 2026 compared to cash used in operations of $2.8 million in the year-ago period.

As of September 30, 2026, Reed's had $2.1 million of cash and $9.25 million of total debt compared to $10.4 million of cash and $9.2 million of total debt net of deferred financing fees as of December 31, 2025. We have taken important steps to strengthen our financial position and balance the company with additional flexibility to execute our growth strategy. On September 30, 2026, we refinanced our $9.25 million senior secured loan, extending its maturity to June 30, 2027, with an option to extend for an additional 3 months, subject to certain conditions.

In addition, subsequent to quarter end, on November 9, 2026, we closed a private placement pursuant to which we issued and sold an aggregate of 17,421,602 shares of common stock for aggregate gross proceeds of $10.0 million. The closing of the transaction satisfied the equity contribution requirement under our amended credit agreement. We intend to use the net proceeds for working capital and other general corporate purposes. This concludes our prepared remarks. Operator, you may open the line for questions.

Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

ByteDance is reportedly working on its own AI agent

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Chinese company wants to compete with Meta’s Muse.

ByteDance wants to take on Meta's Muse with its own AI agent. The company is readying an AI assistant that could be revealed in "the coming weeks," Bloomberg reports.

The report doesn't detail what the agent will be called or how it will work, but says that it will "serve a similar role" to Meta's Muse. Muse has already drawn more than 10 million users and CEO Mark Zuckerberg has predicted the AI agent could generate more revenue per user than its existing "family" of apps. The agent is also expected to play a significant role in Meta's AI glasses, and the company has previewed a standalone "charm" for Muse.

It's not clear if ByteDance is also planning on incorporating its agent into hardware. The AI assistant is one piece of the Chinese company's strategy to compete with frontier AI companies. ByteDance cofounder Zhang Yiming recently told employees that it would likely take "until at least 2029 or 2030" for the company's AI efforts to catch up, Bloomberg reported. Within the AI space, ByteDance has been primarily known for its video generator, Seedance. Clips from the video model went viral on social media platforms earlier this year, which eventually drew legal scrutiny from US-based studios.

If the TikTok maker does introduce its own AI agent, it would also likely raise new concerns about data privacy and how much access personal agents should get. ByteDance didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

For Singles Day, World explains why real is the new attractive

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Singles’ Day falls on 11 November each year and has become the world’s biggest online shopping event.

New Harris Poll research on behalf of World finds that 81% of singles would be more likely to use a dating app if every profile were confirmed as a real person.

As millions of singles celebrate Singles Day on November 11, the biggest question in online dating is no longer “are we compatible?” but “are you even real?”

A new survey conducted by The Harris Poll on behalf of World shows that trust has become the most important factor in online dating. Nearly 3 in 4 online daters (74%) say they have suspected that someone they matched with on a dating app was not a real person. Catfishing, AI-generated profiles, bots and romance scams have turned swiping into a guessing game, and singles say they are tired of it.

World’s answer is Proof of Human: a way for anyone to show they are a real, unique person online without revealing who they are.

The survey paints a clear picture of how much doubt singles now bring to every match:

In other words, being real now outranks looks. For a growing number of singles, authenticity is the new chemistry.

Singles are not giving up on dating apps. They are asking for proof. According to the research, 81% would be more likely to use a dating app if every profile were confirmed as belonging to a real person.

They are also willing to do their part. Four in five singles (80%) say they would take steps to prove there is a real person behind their own profile, as long as their matches did the same. Verification, in other words, is becoming a two-way expectation rather than a burden.

World ID is a universal set of human Proofs that lets people show they are a real, unique person online without sharing personal data such as their name, birthday or address. It is built for a world where AI can generate convincing faces, voices and conversations at scale.

The core of the system is the Orb, a spherical verification device. A person stands in front of it, and the Orb uses images of the eyes and face to confirm they are a unique, living human. That check is turned into an anonymous cryptographic credential, a verified World ID, stored in the World ID App. The process does not require a name, email or gender.

A few features make the technology a natural fit for dating:

The result is a simple promise for singles: the person on the other end of the chat is real.

The largest names in dating are moving in this direction. World first partnered with Match Group, the parent company of Tinder, on a World ID pilot in Japan in 2025. In April 2026, at World’s Lift Off event, the partnership expanded to the United States.

Tinder users who verify with World ID can display a human badge on their profile, signaling to potential matches that a verified, real person is behind it. Verification is optional.

Dating is only one use case. World ID is also being integrated into video calls, document signing, live event ticketing and gaming, with partners including Zoom and Docusign. The common thread is the same one singles describe in the survey: in the age of AI, knowing you are dealing with a real human is becoming essential.

The survey was conducted online in the United States by The Harris Poll on behalf of World from August 20 to 21, 2026, among 892 single adults aged 18 and over, of whom 649 have used online dating apps. Results are accurate to within +/- 3.6 percentage points at a 95% confidence level.

Primary Wave and Kobalt CEOs Discuss Their Billion-Dollar Partnership — And Why ‘You’ll See a Lot More’ Consolidation in Music

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In the latest episode of Billboard On the Record, Larry Mestel and Laurent Hubert talk about Primary Wave's acquisition of Kobalt publicly for the first time.

In February, Billboard broke the news that Primary Wave was nearing a historic deal to acquire Kobalt, arguably the world’s largest independent frontline publisher. Together, it was estimated that the newly combined company would be worth a staggering $7 billion in assets.

By July, the deal finally closed. But given that no one from the two companies had yet spoken to the press, major questions remained about how this new arrangement would operate in practice. All that was disclosed at the time was that Kobalt would operate independently of Primary Wave, one of the largest owners of iconic music catalogs in the industry, with a roster spanning from Whitney Houston to Stevie Nicks to Bob Marley to Prince.

But would Primary Wave move all of its catalogs to Kobalt? Did the deal include assets from Kobalt’s $700m catalog fund with Morgan Stanley? And how do Kobalt’s other verticals fit into the picture? The independent publisher is also home to AMRA, a global digital rights collection service that aims to speed up royalty collection and cut out middlemen outside of the U.S., and Kosign, its digital-first publishing service for middle-class songwriters who are not ready to sign a traditional deal.

News of the acquisition also came at a time of seismic change in the music industry. Universal Music Group’s Virgin division was finalizing its acquisition of Downtown Music Holdings and BMG was moving toward its purchase of Concord, reshaping the state of independent music in a matter of months.

To explain Primary Wave and Kobalt’s new deal for the first time and why all of this consolidation is happening now, Primary Wave founder/CEO Larry Mestel and Kobalt CEO Laurent Hubert joined Billboard’s music business podcast, On the Record.

To watch this episode of Billboard On the Record, check out the YouTube video below or click here for more viewing and listening options. An edited and condensed excerpt of the conversation can also be found below.

When did you guys start discussing this deal?

Hubert: I think the beginning of the conversation was some time in the summer of 2025. I think the first conversation was not a direct conversation with me but with another party at a particular dinner.

Mestel: I remember that dinner, and Primary Wave has historically been administration-agnostic. We’re into the value creation business, not the administration business. So we’ve always used Kobalt or Universal or some other administrators instead of creating our own internal administration platform.

The dinner Laurent was speaking about was a dinner that I believe Goldman Sachs had organized. I remember at that dinner, Jenny Kim, who is head of music now at Goldman, came up to me and said, “Have you considered Kobalt as an acquisition and as a partner?” And I said, “Well, we’re administration-agnostic, but I’ve known Laurent forever. Culturally, I think we’re very similar companies.” She said, “Well, you’re going to consider it because I’m bringing you the deal.” And so I said, “Great, let’s consider it.”

At the end of the day, it made a lot of sense for us because you could never have on your team too many great managers, and Laurent’s team are fantastic managers. We saw some jewels inside of Kobalt, not just the administration platform, which is really fantastic, but divisions like AMRA and Kosign, too.

Why not do administration in-house at Primary Wave?

Mestel: For us, the focus has always been on marketing, on branding, on creating content, doing Broadway, doing movies, doing immersive experiences, creating cosmetic lines, you know, gaming, etc. So there’s a real core competency to that, and it’s very hard for other companies that are competitors to recreate what we recreate. To build an administration platform takes a significant amount of capital and takes a significant amount of effort. And we always viewed it as a commodity, where there were a lot of industry players that did administration, but not many — in fact, I can’t even name any — that does really great marketing and value creation. So we’ve always stayed away from the administration side of the business because we could always rent it at relatively insignificant cost.

You’ve said before that Kobalt is going to stand on its own, but you guys have complementary strengths. Where are the two companies going to work together?

Hubert: I have to credit Larry for the vision for the business because initially, when we first talked, I understood the interest, but I did not fully understand the vision. And then I think Larry explained that very clearly, saying, “Hey, we understand Kobalt. We understand what it means in the market and that it’s a destination today.” He says also he built a destination — which I think is very unique — and we create value differently. You know, for Primary Wave, it’s really about how do you create engagement on the properties that they acquired, and for us, it’s how do we unlock greater value from a collection perspective.

From that perspective, we saw a direct opportunity, and some of it will be that we will cross some functionality that we have, and we’ll compound those benefits without talking about merger or integration. That was also interesting for us from a management perspective because we felt we didn’t have to go through an education process about Kobalt. We don’t have to be disrupted day one. We could keep going and do what we do the best we can do.

Larry, you mentioned that there were some jewels within Kobalt that you were super interested in. You mentioned AMRA, and it’s interesting because I’ve heard similar phrasing back when Francisco Partners purchased Kobalt in 2022. They called AMRA an “unpolished gem.” So I’m curious why you think it’s a gem as well?

Mestel: Those that aren’t using AMRA are going local. How long does it take for you to collect your money? Eight months, 12 months, 15 months. AMRA is going direct, and it’s very hard to recreate AMRA because of the history of AMRA and the licenses that they’ve put in place. So, it’s touching your money sooner, and there are less people with their hands in your pocket. It’s really that simple. I think the more people discover AMRA, the more people are going to want to use AMRA. We have $7.5 billion worth of assets now within Primary Wave. We’re going to be putting as much of our repertoire through AMRA as we possibly can.

Larry, do you plan to switch all the Primary Wave catalogs over to do publishing admin through Kobalt now as well?

Mestel: We have some great partners around the world, and I’ll name two: Kobalt and Universal. We give Universal quite a bit of our administration worldwide. They are an excellent administration organization. We have a great relationship with Jody [Gerson, CEO of UMPG] and Mark [Cimino, COO of UMPG] and David [Kokakis, chief counsel of UMPG] and the team there. Kobalt will be getting much more of Primary Wave repertoire than it has gotten in the past, but we won’t be taking anything away that we’ve given Universal, and we’ll continue to give Universal more work in the future because they’ve done a great job.

I guess you also can’t just stop in the middle of an administration agreement with another company, like UMPG, just because you bought Kobalt this year.

Mestel: We have very short-term deals, but we’re not going to do that. The reason is that when you do short-term deals, you keep partners engaged. If you put up a long-term deal, maybe there’s less incentive. So I like to do month-to-month, quarter-to-quarter, year-to-year. I don’t like much more than year-to-year.

We are very well capitalized, and the fact that we’re very well-capitalized means we don’t need advances because when you take advances, that means you lower your margin. I’d rather have a much higher margin, no advance.

Luminate CEO Rob Jonas recently came on this podcast and revealed that now 150,000 songs are being uploaded to streaming services every day. With the sheer volume of music now, how has that changed your approach?

Mestel: I think that’s much more relevant for Laurent than it is for me because we are in the icon and legend business. We’re buying only the greatest of the great.

Hubert: Frankly, in the past 10-15 years, the way we manage publishing has totally transformed. We went from an ownership to a consumption model. Obviously, we went from a few hundred income sources to literally thousands of income sources.

We also have seen fragmentation of copyright. It’s not uncommon to see five, seven writers on a song, and you have fragmentation of consumption. So if you look at those factors, it has compounded volume in [many] ways, and the challenge with volume is you need to manage that with great velocity because we have to account every quarter. [Just] because you have more to manage [doesn’t mean] that you can delay your accounting period, obviously. Plus, you also have [more] challenges with accuracy. Our job as we build the infrastructure — it’s a continuous exercise — is, how do we manage this?

I’ve often had a conversation with my CTO: How do we manage [Amazon Web Services] cost? All of it is in the cloud. You have volume growing at 30%-40% a year, and you have your cost of AWS growing at 10%-12% a year, and we have to manage the business. You have to find ways to, again, optimally manage that ecosystem without affecting velocity. It’s frankly a job of every day. It really is. It’s a natural tension in the business.

So, are publishers spending more than ever to try to collect all these royalties all over the world?

Hubert: We are. The challenge is that the value of that micro license or micro transaction is so small that you have to do it cost-effectively; otherwise you’d be losing money [to collect it]. Technology is obviously a major aspect of what we do. AI is, of course, but it’s not only AI — it’s about building infrastructure that can handle the volume. It’s a challenge. This is a topic that we talk about almost every day.

Kobalt announced a $700 million fund in 2023 to buy music catalogs. Is that part of this deal, or do you retain that at Kobalt?

Hubert: We retain [it], and that’s a conversation that I had [with Larry] because we have a different IP strategy and we deploy on different type of copyrights. For us, the idea here was to provide an additional service to our clients, and we provided all the services under a lending situation. But if people needed a capital event, we wanted to be able to say yes, we can do that for you as well. We created a fund in ’11 and a second fund in ’16. We created that fund in 2023, and we’ve been deploying the capital, obviously, not only on clients that we have because we know them and they come to us, but we’re also buying third-party copyrights.

Mestel: We turn down many more deals than we do, and so Laurent’s team is participating in our deal meetings. When there’s a deal that we’re reviewing that isn’t right for Primary Wave, we’re turning it over to Kobalt [now].

Larry, I think one of the most interesting things Primary Wave has done from a marketing perspective is the Whitney Houston slot machines in Las Vegas. How are those going? Are they a success?

Mestel: It has been a giant success for us. We started this in 2024, and we budgeted 200 machines a year. Now, we’ve done over 500 a year. We just renewed the deal with [slot machine manufacturer] IGT. We are having great success, but how do you measure success? Well, you can measure success on shipping machines and shipping more than your budget, but the real measure is that we’re making more money for the estate of Whitney Houston, which we own 50% of, [on slot machines] than the entire estate was generating six and a half years ago when we bought into the estate on that one deal.

Larry, you often talk about how you guys partner with the estates when you do these deals, but I know estates can be really sensitive about certain brand partnerships. Like, anything that involves sex, alcohol or even gambling. What was that conversation like with the Houston estate to decide that this was right for her legacy?

Mestel: It was easy. Whitney loved to play slot machines. It was very organic. We will not do a brand deal, a marketing deal, any type of brand alliance, movie, anything unless the artist estate 100% signs off and says yes.

Willard Ahdritz, founder of Kobalt, is stepping away from his role as chairman as part of this deal. Who is now filling that role?

Mestel: The reality is there’s only one person that matters, which is the CEO, which is Laurent. We were clear with each other day one that the reason why Primary Wave was doing this, a big chunk of the reason, was the management team in Laurent and Kobalt. It is an entrepreneurial company. Laurent runs it with very little interference at all from Primary Wave. We talk a couple times a week, so we’re very active on what we’re doing together. Kobalt is and will remain a separate entrepreneurially-run company.

Both Primary Wave and Kobalt have active funds to buy more catalogs. Kobalt’s is a $700 million fund, announced in 2023 with Morgan Stanley, and Primary Wave’s is a $2.2 billion fund with Brookfield, announced in the spring. How much of these funds have you both deployed so far?

Mestel: We are way ahead of schedule. We’ve deployed almost 60% of our most current fund, which we only closed back in April of this year. There have been some very, very large-scale deals, and our pipeline is pretty significant. So we anticipate being probably 75% invested at the end of the year and fully invested the first quarter of next year. We have many pools of capital. We have a permanent evergreen fund with Brookfield. Brookfield put $2 billion on [the] primary balance sheet four and a half years ago, whenever that was, 2022, and we have a lot of surprises.

Hubert: We’ve deployed about half a billion dollars of the $700 million, and we intend to spend the [remainder] of that $200 million going forward. So there will be a new fund [soon] … [Primary Wave and Kobalt] don’t go after the same targets at all. We typically buy more recent vintages. We buy copyrights where we have high incentives of digital royalties because we want to take advantage of the AMRA capabilities.

2026 has been a year full of major music business acquisitions. UMG’s Virgin Music Group acquired Downtown, BMG bought Concord and Primary Wave acquired Kobalt. On a macro scale, why do you think these acquisitions and this consolidation is happening now?

Mestel: I think you can see a lot more. I think as interest rates go up, I think you will see a lot of our competitors that are financially driven looking to sell. Primary Wave, by the way, has no debt, so we’re generating significant returns without that because of the alpha we’re creating as we market. A lot of our competitors are highly leveraged, so they’re taking very safe assets that generate cash flow every single day that would normally go back out into acquisition, or go back out in the form of dividends to investors. These companies are paying interest to banks, and as interest rates go up and they have to refinance their significant debt, they’re going to have a hard time. I don’t name competitors, but you’re going to see a lot of people that are going to be selling, I think, over the course of the next two and three years. So you’ll see a lot more consolidation, and that’s great for companies like Kobalt and Primary Wave, especially ones that are very well capitalized and have almost no debt.

But is it better for artists and songwriters?

Mestel: Well, it’s better if you’re with a company that has an infrastructure to create content for them and to actually market [their music]. Is it any good when an artist is acquired by a private equity firm? Probably not, because the first thing on their mind isn’t legacy protection, and it’s not turning a hit song that was a hit 30 years ago into a hit song again. It’s, how are they going to extract as much cash out of the asset as they possibly can, and then likely flip it? So yeah, I think it’s very good if the artist is being acquired by a company that has the ability to do something.

SpaceX Wants To Build Data Center Powered By Its Newest Product: Natural Gas

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SpaceX filed its intention to build a new AI data center at its facility in Starbase, Texas, which will be powered by a new natural gas plant that it will also build. None of that sounds like space, but this is the company that SpaceX has become. The initial phase of this build-out is projected to cost $24 billion, though more phases are to come. Critically, this gives the rocket company, which is also an internet company, which is also an AI company, which is also a social media company, a way to make use of its newest product: natural gas. Again, space!

The company has been moving towards an expansion into fossil fuels for the last few months. In June, SpaceX president Gwynne Shotwell said the company was making plans for "basically mining our own natural gas... to develop our own propellant and bring it to the rocket." The rocket in question being Starship, which SpaceX is essentially betting its future on. That takes a mixture of methane and liquid oxygen to fire up its thrusters; methane can be extracted from natural gas. For that reason, the company has made major moves to ensure its own supply. It hired for its own natural gas trader, normally an oil industry job for buying and selling the stuff, and is building its own pipelines in both Florida and Texas, per Bloomberg.

In addition, SpaceX rapidly spun up AI data centers in Tennessee and Mississippi earlier this year, powered by, you guessed it, natural gas. It spun them up so rapidly that the company is now being sued for ignoring environmental laws. SpaceX is apparently trying to go fast again for this new Texas endeavor, likely trying to get things rolling before a newer, bluer state government takes over. SpaceX wants to grab both the air pollution permits it needs plus a few tax breaks; Texas is expected to make both harder to get early next year, per Politico. To that end, Starbase's city government will convene on the matter in December. There's good reason to think the company will get what it wants: two of the city's three commission members are SpaceX executives. That's handy.

As for the power plant itself, it will feature 35 mobile natural gas turbines and produce around 750 megawatts of electricity. So it's now in the business of building power plants, buying and trading natural gas, and laying out pipelines, all in service of the enough data centers it's putting up. No wonder Vice President of Launch Kiko Dontchev is now saying, "Fundamentally we are an infrastructure company at this point." You don't say, Dontchev.

At this point, it's not too much of a stretch to say that SpaceX is just the Elon Musk Show, going into whatever industry he feels like at the moment. If his long-desired merger with Tesla goes through, then it will really just be a conglomerate with all sorts of fingers in all sorts of pies. Space will still be a part of what SpaceX does, but now that AI is earning it more revenue than rockets, it may become a smaller and smaller part. Mars has never looked so far away.

Teradata: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Teradata's sovereign cloud push targets banks and governments that cannot send data to public clouds, a market where on-premise control may decide the contract.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Teradata (TDC +1.65%) raised its full-year earnings guidance and doubled its target for annual operating margin expansion. Expanding operating margins while investing in artificial intelligence (AI) software demonstrates operational discipline.

Converting customer pilot programs into recurring software revenue will determine long-term capital compounding. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Management raised full-year non-generally accepted accounting principles, or non-GAAP, earnings per share (EPS) guidance to a range of $2.86 to $2.90. Teradata also increased full-year operating margin expansion guidance. Discipline in sales and administrative expenses allowed the business to fund software research while improving profitability.

Government and banking clients increasingly require physical control over data locations to meet regulatory rules. Integrating graphics processing units, or GPUs, directly into on-premise hardware lets customers run complex analytical models without sending data to public clouds.

This is why the company launched Teradata Factory, which is a unified computing environment that brings data, large language model software, and AI agents into on-premises infrastructure to provide physical control over AI execution.

Enterprise software customers hesitate to expand automated tasks if computing costs scale out of control. Management launched Tera, an automated coworker tool for data tasks, to lower computing overhead and speed up execution.

Recurring revenue grew 4% year-to-date, providing predictable cash flows while full-year adjusted free cash flow outlook rose to $340 million at the midpoint. Control over on-premise infrastructure combined with sovereign cloud partnerships gives the company room to expand installed accounts without losing software pricing leverage.

Strategic focus remains on converting artificial intelligence experimentation into production workloads across financial services and government clients. Software flexibility positions the platform to capture complex enterprise workloads.
